CAPITAL" CHAT.

Sir Robert Rawlinson has never forgotten that in early life he was a stonemason. On one occasion„ while passing through Chorley with a friend, he pointed to a step and said. "That step is well-set. don't you think e " Row should I know ?" re- torted his companion—a well-knownlawyer, by the way. "I do," was Sir Robert's rejoinder, "because I set it myself."

Ibsen must be a very singular person, and cidedly superstitious. lie confesses that he could not get inspiration during his writing if there was not a tray befcire him which contained a little bear in wood, a little black devil holding a wax candle, and several little rabbits and cats made of copper. "This may appear to you to be ridiculous,' said Ibsen," but so it is. As to the use I make ol them, that is my secret, and I shall not divulge it to you or anyone else."

The Russian Empress is making remarkable pro- gress in Russian. flowerer, not being a very. good French linguist, the Empress speaks G.,rman and English with her consort's entourage. !LT Majesty is passionately fond.of MUM, and practices on the piano several hours a day. Stie is now in search of a young lady of the Russian aristocracy as a musical companion, and there are of course, scores of appli- cants for this enviable position.

Probably nobody in the world worships Mr Gladstone more than Mr. George Armitstead, with whom he so often travels abroad. " Cieorge" is an extremely wealthy man and a Russian merchant at Dundee, which city he represented for many years in Parliament He is never so happy as wnen en- tertaining his hero and saving him all the tremble incidental to a tour abroad. Mr. Armitstead's house in Cleveland-square is usually Mr. Herbert Glad- stone's London home.

Mr. Lloyd George, M.P., is a young man of a little over thirty, and from an elocutionist's point, of view one of the finest speakers in the House of Commons. He is not very tall, and looks a great deal younger than he really is, and wnen he speaks, not one of his ideas is ever lost or weakened for want of words. His fiery Celtic eloquence and perfect delivery are, it is said,to a great extent duo to the fact that he studied under the great Wel,ll preachers. Ile is passionately fond of music and singing.

Albert Grimaldi, Prince of Monaco, has broken faith with several of the foreign Courts of Europe by having received many hundreds of thousands of pounds as his share of the profits at the Monte Carlo tables. The Prince, after his marriage to the enormously wealthy daughter of the New Orleans and Parisian banker, Heine, had given a promise not TO derive any further revenue from the gambl- ing-tables. He gave it to be understood that he was bound by contract made by his father to cen- tinue to accord shelter to the cenipany for a certain number of years, but that he would neither renew , the lease nor participate in the profits of the tables. It wagon this belief that Que-n Victoria consented to accord sovereign honours to himself and to his present wife on the occasion of their visit to Eng- land. He has not even ancient lineage to recom- mend him, his grandmother, Marie Louise Gilbert, being the daughter of a pork butcher, while his grandfather, before being called to succeed his distant cousin on the throne ot Monaco as Florestan the First, was a fourth-rate actor at the Ambigu Theatre at Paris.

A recent visitor to Cap Martin describes Mr. Gladstone as being in excellent health and high spirits. The operation for cataract ha 8 proved quite successful. Except when he reads or writes, and sometimes then, be is freed from the obligation ot using spectacles. He is able. when he thinks it necessary, to conduct his correspondence with his own hand.

By way, perhapa, of compensating for a night whose dulness was forseen, the proceedings within the House of Commons commenced on Tuesday with one of those bits of picturekque pageantry which occasionally light up its sombre walls, and give a dash of borrowed brilliancy to its everyday gloom. The Corporation of Dublin having adopted a petition praying for an amnesty to the political prisoners, the Lord Mayor was hidden to .brave the. p,rils of the stormy seas in order to exercise the privilegeotpresenting the document in person At' the bar of the House. Accordingly this alter- noon the Lord Mayor, supported by sundry ' aldermen and officials arrayed in gorgeous robes, demanded admission, and, by command of the Speaker, was bidden to enter " My Lord Mayor of Dublin." The Speaker thereupon demanded in his most awe-inspiring tones," What have you there ?" Standing at the bar, flanked by his companionsoind jealously 'guarded .by the Sergeant-at-Arms with the mace on his shoulder, the Lord Mayor made a clear bremt of his dire secret. He recited the terms of the petition with oratorical fervour. The docu- ment was handed in, the Speaker gravely inclined his head, and the Municipality of Dublin, having thuS impressively delivered its message, bowed it- sell out.

There is a great concern among the Radicals be- low the gangway in regard to the tone of the replies made by the Chancellor of the Exchequer on the subject of the payment of members at Tuesday's sitting. A meeting of the Radical Committee was held, after questions to consider the matter, Mr. Storey being in the chair. A resolution was carried expressing deep regret that the Govertiment had not seen their way to redeem the pledges given last session. It has been forwarded to Sir William Har- court,and the subject will probably be raised in the House at an early opportunity. I have some reason to think, however, that the replica of the Chancellor of the Exchequer have been misinterpreted, and that the Government are not so doubtful as to the possibility of dealing with this question as appears to be supposed.

I hear very gloomy reports as to the position in which the Act of last year is likely to leave the railway rates question. The Act is obscurely drawn: and, in spite of constant warnings, many traders have clearly failed to grasp the essential point that unless all complaints and claims for repayment of other charges were actually in the bands of the Board of Trade before the 25th instant the railway companies will have in any CasB abso- lutely secured the whole of the increases In their charges levied or claimed by them since 1892. It is not too much to say that a more danger.vus and ineffective Act has rarely been passed in connection with trading interests. The mere fact that each individual rate has to be separately complained of anti decided by the Railway Commissioners, proves how hopeless the case of the trades will be unless they act, wiih promptitude and en it scale they have not dreamed of before now. The railway companies are delighted with the situation. They are confident that in a vast proportion of cases they will in the next few months have made good for ever the advances of 5 per cent on the 1892 rates, which it has been their aim from the first to secure as a set-af for the alleged lonsee they sus- tained from the new classification of merchandise under the Act of 1888.

The career of Dr. Joseph Parry has been a very varied one. Burn of poor parents at Mart hyr Tydvil, with a heritage of labour, but a talent for music, he bad, at the tender age of ten years. to work at the puddling furnaces. In 1854, when thirteen years of age, he went to America, from which his family soon returned to their native land. It was then, ap- parently, that young Parry begau to get Kane systematic musical instruction. and in 1862 he won prizes at the Llandudno Eisteddfod. After winning bronze and silver medahs, he was appointed Proles- :or of Music at the University College,Aberystwith. in 1871, taking his degree of Mus. Bac. at Cam- bridge, soon after. In 1878 he obtained his dcctorate.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY FEBRUARY 16. 1895.

LADIES' LETTER.

My DZAREST NELLIZ,

It seems rather early as yet to talk of spring, but you will do well to prepare for it. As soon as 'the days begin to get a little brighter, we shall want something light-looking in the way of an outdoor wrap. Far this purpose, I think there is nothing nicer than a velvet cape. The velvet should be of a bright colour, trimmed with fur. There are few materials better than velvet for wearing when one wants to be warmly clad, and, at the same time, to have a really bright, smart-looking garment. The cloak which I have described could be lined with a quilted silk, or might have a thicknets of demob between it and the liming, so as to make it warm enough to keep out the keen, penetrating winds of early spring. Another cape which I like very much, and which has a certain amount of addi- tional warmth, is made with a sleeveless bodice underneath, and the cape just opening in the front to show the fur edging of the under todice. One of the most fashionable bats is the Louis XVI., a broad-brimmed model with drooping sides, turned up Eat at the back with a large bow holding a plume of upright ostrich feathers. But I must warn you that it is a little difficult to wear. It can be of felt or velvet, the edge 'trimmed with a bias band of ,s%tin or jet passementerie. This bat is only fib for dressy wear, being a little too flighty for Morning walks, when the pliant felt hat, with bow, and wing at the side, and crushed-in crown, or the felt sailor hat trimmed with a taffetas drapery, is more suitable and becoming. Nothing can be prettier, though, than a hat known as the Maurice hat, which is made in fancy pink straw. The brim is out away at the back, and replaced by a cluster of roses shaded from pink to crimson. There is an under brim of pleated black lace, and a shaded pink glace silk bow in front on the crown. The ornaments are In jet.

There are some delightful opera wraps to be seen jest now. A very handsome one is composed of pink cloth with olive green velvet and fi x fur for trim. ming. The pelisse should be lined throughout with earcenet, and is slightly shaped at the underarm learn. It is gathered to a square yoke hack and front, the join ooncealed with the fur trimmings. It fastens invisibly with hooks In the centre of front. The neck is finished with a high•shaped collar of velvet, trimmed with fur. The plain sleeve foundation is Need from wrist to elbow with the cloth, to the edge of this the full sleeve is gathered, and again into the armhole, with an epaulette of the cloth lined with • arcenet and edged with fur. A velvet gauntlet cuff edged with the same gives a smart finish to the wrist. Another, of very much the am° design, can be used as an evening and travelling cloak combined. This useful mantle can be made in soveral colours, lined throughout with silk, and interlined for extra warmth.

Another fashi.)nable gown (a tea-gown) is of mauve gaufred hemmed at the edge with a band of Florentine lace. The front is without breast-seams, so that it falls straight, covered from the neck to below the waist with a point or bertha of lace, which Is joined by the lace epaulets on the bust beneath two large bows. This would be an exquisite style for Liberty's pale coloured velveteens. I quite forgot to tell you of a really charming little cape I saw the day helots yesterday. It was composed of coloured silk velvet, trimmed with Russian lace (or black jet, to taste), with rolled oollas of ortroohan or fur.

Pretty clovers for bor.km of all kinds can be made' from coarse white or 6.ru linen, the title of the: volume for which the case is intended haing out-1 lined with gold thread, and the whole of the back- ground darned—not too finely—with yellow flax, the letters alone bling untoneh%i; or the inscrip- tion can be worked solid with flax or silk, and th 3 ground dotted all o or with little round gilt spangles sewn don fiat at rather oleos intervals, the edges of the cover being finished off with couple of rows of narrow gold braid. Yours lovingly, JaNras

There is one very good explanation of the fact th tt great cities almost invariably grow towards the west. As regards Europe, the prevailing winds are from the west and south-west, fini that these portions of the towns are brighter, cleaner, and healthier than the eastern. Cockroaches are never wittingly slain by Chinamen. They consider them sacred insects, and think it por- tends ill luck to step on them. As they never make any effort to exterminate them, the Chinese quarters are usually overrun with these pests. One of the cleverest inventions ever passed by the American patent office is the machine for sticking Common pins in the papers in which they are sold. The contrivance brings up the pins in rows, draws the paper in position, crimps it in two lines, then at a single push passes the pins through the paper and sets them in position. • Miss Ada Heather-Bigg was one of the pioneers of the Children's "Happy Evening " movement, says the "Spinning Wheel, arid perhaps no lady living has done more to brighten the lives of the little ones in the Board Schools of the great city. The Children's Happy Even- ing Association was formed at the suggestion of Miss Heather-Bieg in 1890. It works in conjunction with the School Board, various Board Schools being thrown open as playrooms. once a fortnight. Various amuse- ments go on in the different class rooms. In the "quiet" room, the children play quiet games, such as draughts, dominoes, card games, drawing, painting, Sec. In another room, fairy tales, perhaps, are being told, while

the larger rooms are given up to more noisy games. Sometimes magic la ntern entertainmentsa.re given. The Happy Evenings movement is a colossal success, and all praise is due to thoie who conceived and have so success- fully carried out the nrok‘et:

Cabbage water should be thrown out immediately it is done with. Pieces of paper moistened with paraffin or turpentine, laid amongs•:, woollen garments or furs, prevents the in-roads of moths. _ .

Powdered bath-brick applied on a cloth will remove the burnt milk from enamelled saucepans and pie- dishes. _ . .

Flour, soap, and candles should be pure based in large quantities, as they improve with keeping. Candles that have'become discoloured may be re stored by rubbing with a flannel cloth slightly dipped in spirits of wine.

When boiling a pudding in a cloth, always place a small plate or saucer in the pan to prevent burning. A little paraffin mixed with blacking gives a good polish to boots withffittle trouble. A taLlsspoonfut of turpentine added to the water in which linen is passed through, after being starched,gives

.„ the linen a good gloss. To make the wick of a lamp burn clear, steep it in strong vinegar and dry thoroughly before immersing in the oil. . . .

Sheets that have worn thin in the centre should not he discarded; they should be cut through, the selvedges joined together, and the sides hemmed. . All brown paper, twine, Sm., that come to the houso should be preserved ; they will be found. useful iii wrap- ping up parcels, &s. To know if a cake is suffisiently baked, thrust a knit- ting needle through the centre; if anything adheree to it, return the cake to the oven for a little longer ; if the needle is clear, the cake is enough.

' Inoigerrtion. . This is a very old enemy which never ceases to plague many of us, and at present the age we live in is said to be an " indigestion age" on a.ecount of the cheapness of fond and the amount consumed by those who least of all requite it. All the besiness of life is now a constant rush and hurry, and the eating of food of an indigestible kind is found to impair the functions of the stomach. Many dyspileics do not show their trouble on their faces, but others present a pimply and reddish counten- ance that is a source of pain to the owner and disagree- able to his or her friends. Many men and women win live a secluded or confined life quite expect to be able to make themselves at home with everything on their dinner table. We can say with all truth that most dyspeptics owe the contrivance of their trouble to their own bad practices. Very often we have been street with the temerity of people whom we know to b- martyrs to indigestion. for they will eat articles of food that would make a strong stomach quail. There is al- ways a cause to be discovered in all cases of indigestion. Some people may suffer-through the food being bolted without proper mastication and admixture of saliva ; some through drinking alcoholic liquors to excess, or such seemingly harmless beverages as tea and coffee ; some through constipation, . depending, perhaps, on close confinetneut to business ; some to imperfect or ex- cessive secretion of the gastric juice; and others to weak action of the muscles of the stoniach, perhaps arising through regular over-distension by gourmaydisine. Everyone who consults his or her doctor should carry out his instructions to the letter. It is our custom to supply a printed dietary to those not gifted with a good memory ; but we are afraid few are aIWaT 9 guided by it. There is no near prospect of cure for the dyspeptic so long as he will take his cheese at night after a dinner that *mild call forth the powers- of a Colossus. The best way to cure yourself of the pain after food, distension, palpitation, and feeling of lassi- tude and weariness that accompany indigestion, is to jot down what you have foetid by experience suits you beat, and what does not, and then to sybtematically ad- here to your home-made dietary. A little carbonate of soda dissolved iu water, taken after meals, relieves heartburn by neutralising the gastric juice. Milk foods constitute the best Piet for most cases ; and, if these do Inot agree, peptordsed milk may be used, or powders con- tainimg pepsin may be added to the food before eating. 'Exercise iu the open air whenever possible, the keeping of early hours as to sleeping and Waking, with care over food talon, and is guard over the pipe, cigar, or cigarette. will : soon make a marked change in the patient affected, and, if ,the treatment is continued, will bring back once more that priceless treasure—good health. _

WE PARTED AT THE ALTAR;

BY LAURA JEAN LIBBEY, Author of "A Mad Betrothal," " "Parted

by Fate," "Floribere Lover," etc., eta.

CHAPTER. I.

"TO THE GRAND BALL.' "Choose not alone a proper mate But proper time to marry." " WHEN you read what I have written here,, I shall be lying cold in death." The words were written by the trembling white hand of a young girl, standing in her bridal robes alone, at midnight, in the vestry of an old, isolated church. • Continuing, she wrote rapidly on the leaf she had torn from the marriage register: . "Despite the shock, it will not matter much to you, although but a few moments since—only a few moments—you led me to the altar, and vowed there to• love and cherish me—oh, cruel words! Oh, false vows !—when you know your heart was old and bitter toward me. "I am going to set you free, knowing I must part from you. lam n going to die ; but death is sweeter than life without you. Yes, at the very altar you shall have your,freedom back again. " Your poor, unhappy, loving Donis." Let us read the romance of the few brief days that led to the love of this ill-mated bride and groom; how they met, and what caused this hasty and most reckless of all reckless marriages ; and why the young bride fled from her bridegroom at the very altar. Only three days previous to the opening of our story, on a sunny June afteinoon; the hands of the great clock on the wall of Madame Delmar's fashionable seminary at Beech grove, Maryland, are slowly creeping around to the closing hour of four, but it seems to the restless, 1)1.4a-eyed seminary-girls that the closing hour will never roll around to-day. The bell taps at last, lessons are over, and a moment later a bevy of young ladies come fluttering, laughing and chatting down the broad stone steps, as only bright, romping, happy light- hearted school-girls—who know nothing of the cares of the world—can laugh and talk. Among the group, yet quite apart from the rest, was a slight, fair young girl, differing from her companions by being far prettier. Her plain muslin dress, reduced almost to shabbiness, was sadly in contrast to their dainty, ruffled mulls; but you would lose sight of this in gazing at the equisite, piquant beauty of the dimple rosebud. face, framedin burled golden hair, and the large blue eyes deeply, beautifully blue, like the heart of a velvet pansy; yet she was only Doris Brandon, madame's dependant ward. Turning abruptly into a side path, Doris crossed the lawn ' with flying footsteps. Reaching a secluded spot at the lower end of the spacious grounds, she flung herself down on the daisy. studded grass, sobbing as though her heart would break. Angry, defiant, rebellious tears they ,were, and surely no young girl ever had more bitter cause to weep. "Oh, dear! oh, dear !" she sobbed, dashing the great pearly dew-drops away with a slim white hand, "if my life were only like the life of other young girls! Oh, it was bitterly cruel of madame to taunt me with my dependence before the whole school to-day! If I only had wings, like this brown linnet in the tree over my head, how quickly I would fly from this dreary prison. lam young, and life runs warm in my veins, fills my heart, beats in every pulse; yet how can I live without even one of those things that make life endurable? How much longer is this monotonous life to last I wonder." It was to end sooner than she knew; this very day was to be the turning point of her life, whether for weal Or for woe, ah ! who shall say? She removed her hat, and the June sunshine fell unheeded on the graceful head crowned with tumbled, golden hair, that framed the flushed, pretty, tear-wet face. A slight wind raised the hat from the grass, and bending forward quickly to recover it, she caught sight of her own face in the clear, babbling brook that flowed through the grounds and on to the glittering Chesapeake. "Ah If that face belonged to any one else it would be called very pretty. It is fairer than Vivian Courtney's and they rave about her beauty. Who could find anything to admire in a poor little nobody like me? I might as well have been ugly, for all the good my beauty does me," sighed Doris. There was a time coming when that same face was to be more brilliant, when crowds were to sur- round it and do homage to its loveliness. But it was never fairer than on this sunlit afternoon when Doris Brandon bent over the stream and moaned for the dreariness of her young life. The far-off shriek of the incoming afternoon ex- press startled her, andlihe sprang to her feet with a little cry. "I had almost forgotten to ;go down to ,the south gate and watch for Vivian Courtney's beau, who is to come by that train, and deliver to hiM the letter she intrusted to me to give him." Picking up her sun-hat; and hastily tying the blue ribbons under her dimpled chin, Doris rose quickly, and glancing furtively beck at the grim seminary walls, sped &way in tho.direction Of the old south gate on her fateful errand. At that moment the Southern express, which was twenty minutes late, steamed into the little station of Beech Grove, on the Chesapeake. From the rear carriage sprang a handsome young man, who gazed abouc him quite doubtfully, a moment after the train steamed on. He is a tall, handsome, well-dressed young fellow, dark as to hair and complexion, and thoroughly aristocratic, from the straw hat which is pushed rather carelessly back from his broad, white brow to the toe of his polished boot, which he is tapping rather impatiently with his gold. mounted, ebony walking-stick. "Ali ! that must be the place," he says to him- self, eying curiously a large atone building with many turrets and gables. "Yes, that niust be Madame Delmar's seminary. Vivian said I couldn't possibly miss it." And for the twentieth time that afternoon he drew from his breast pocket a dainty, perfumed, pink-tinted, monogramed letter, which was d irected in a girlish hand to "Mr FREDERICK THORNTON, JE., care of Mr. Thornton, Esquire, Banker, No. Wall-street, New York." He drew the dainty letter from the envelope, and looked carefully at the directions in the post- script again : "Take the path to the right of the station, and It will lead you directly to the seminary, by way of the old south gate. I will be there waiting for you. Be sure you come in time for the grand ball I have set my heart on going. It will be a wonder- ful affair. All the girls of the school are just wild over it, and can scarcely wait for Tuesday evening to roll around. I must close now, in order to watch my opportunity to smuggle this into the mailbag and outwit madame, whose eyes are over on the alert to prevent billets-doux from leav- tag her establishment. " Yow a in great. haste, " VIVIAN COURTNEY." "Pretty, wilful Vivian," he mused, with a smile and a flush on his handsome face. "Before I leave Beech Grove, you will have to answer 'yes' or 'no' to a certain-question that, has been agitating my heart this many a (lay." Ile Walked quickly up the daisy-bordered path, thinking of the bright, girlish eyes that would be watching for him from one of the dormitory :Windows. , Turning an abrupt curve in the path, he came suddenly in sight of the white, arched gate of the seminary grounds ; and standing beneath the tall 'arch, under the waving plumes of a lilac tree, was a picture he never forgot while his life lasted—a pie- ture that would have st.lttled any young man who was a beauty-worshipper ; and with the sight, all thoughts of Vivian Ilew from his mind. At the first-rapid glance he had beheld a slim young, girl in is blue dotted muslin dress, a bewitchingly pretty face, half shaded by a broad straw hat, and waving, golden hair, a small, red, smiling mouth, and a pair of wonder- ful blue eyes. He approached, raising his straw hat with a low bow "I expected to see Miss Courtney here," he said, rather confusedly for this debonair, worldly young man. 4.. n -.4 am here in Vivian Courtney's place, or iather, to deliver a letter to you from her, if you are Mr. Frederick Thornton, and I suppoie you are," she .interrupted eagerly. He smiled amusedly, and bowed, and she drew from the folds of her pocket a tiny little note which she placed in his hands. She was jwit turning to leave him, when lie said, eagerly; "Please don't go yet. This may require an . answer."

any glances now and cnen from under her long lashes at the face of the handsome young stranger as he perused her schoolmate's note. Doris Brandon had never seen such a handsome young man before. The French masters and the music professors of the seminary were cros,i and very ugly. The doctor and the rector, who came occasionally, were both old, and the few young men in the village that lay over the hill were very corn- Thou-place sort of persons, inileed. No. wonder this smiling, handsome young man quite captivated Doris's girlish fancy at first sight. The note which Vivian had written contained but a few tear-blotted words, and was as follows: "DEAR FREDERICK :—Father has Come quite unexpectedly, and I am to go home with him, he says. We have barely" time to reach the train. Oh, how disappointed you will be when you come. I am, oh, so sorry. Fate seems against us. What a pity it is to miss the ball, too, after I had set my heart on going. I hope I shall not lose all. that I set my heart on: I shall have to get some one of the school girls to meet you and deliver this to you. I don't know who yet. "There is no answer," said Mr. Thornton, smil- ing; and adding imploringly: "But won't you sit down on this log for a moment, and tell me, please, how it was that Vivian left the seminary so hastily, and what she said to you when she intrusted you with this note." _ _ _

Blushing prettily, and thinking it would be uite ill•mannered to refuse him, Doris sat down. "Vivian's father came quite unexpectedly or her," she said, quite unconscious that she was repeating the words of the note, and that he knew all about it already, "and just as she was going down the steps with her father she thrust two notes into my ham!; one was tome and read: "'Give the other note to a young man who is to come on the afternoon train and be at the old south gate at four o'clock this afternoon. Wittch sharp for him, and don't let madame see or know. Of course I know it's a little bit wrong to deceive madame, but oh, it's so romantic, you know.' Frederick Thornton smiled again as he stood leaning carelessly against the trunk of a tree, watching her. "What a sweet, guileless little creature she is," be thought. At that moment a sharp, rasping voice broke harshly on the summer air, calling loudly: "Doris I Doris Brandon, where are you? I want you I" It was droll to see how • the birds flew out of their nests, gave a terrified whirl back again, singing loudly to each other,. as though they would "' "Look! look! Here in our green, shady para- dise is an angry woman I" • Doris sprang from her mossy seat in alarm. "It is Madame Delmar," she cried, in affright. "How angry she would be with me if she found me loitering here." With a little nod of her curly bead, she would have sprung down the shady pail, but Frederick Thornton put out his white hand detainingly. A sudden impulse came to him to see this fair young girl again, and that was the beginning of the fatal end.

"Will you come here to-morrow afternoon at this time for a no' e to deliver to Vivian when she returns to school?" lie asked earnestly. . . . .

"Yes,' she promised, hasiily, and In an instant she was lost to sight among the trees. "Doris Brandon. Au, what a pretty name, and what a pretty young girl. But somehow, she does not look quite happy-," he mused, as he turned away with something vary like a sigh. Doris eluded madame by taking another path to the seminary. Her little heart was in a strange whirl and a dark smiling face seemed to dance between her and the sunlight. Poor child ! She would have had a happy enough life of it if her path hal never been crossed by this handsome young man.

• CHAPTER THE LOVERS.

FREDERICK THORNTON paced up and down the lane before the old eouth gale, quite hzdf an hour the next afternoon before Doris made her appearance.

"I was afraid you had quite forgotten your promise to come here to-day, Duds. You will pardon me for calling Toil that; it sounds so much sweeter than to say Bliss'Brandon,' he added.

"I should hardly know 'bow to answer to the name of Miss Brandon," she said, thoughtfully. "You are the first person who ever addressed me so; every one calls use Doris; I like it best. As to roy coming late, madame was very cross with, me to-day. I could not get away befors." Ah, how sorry he looked. "I heard over at the village that the present term at the seminary closed yesterday afternoon, and most of the young ladies had returned to their respective homes. I feared you had gone after I bad seen you," he said; "and as to the note, it was useless to write it in that case.' "You thought I was a pupil here" she asked, laughing softly, white the colour came and went in her pretty, dimpled cheeks " Yea, I thought so," he answered. "Is it not so ?" "Oh, dear no," she answered, with a merry, girlish laugh. "Jam only Madame Delmar's ward. I have lived here always.' "Always ?" he repeated, wonderingly. She nodded her curly head. He was a stranger in that locality or he would not have asked that question. Every one in the village knew Doris's history—how, seventeen years before, a stranger, heavil veiled, had been seen making her way in the dusky twilight through the village streets, pausing now and then to ask a stray pedestrian which road led to "the young ladies seminary." Each one that she spoke to noticed that she carried a heavy, dark basket, and that her voice was low and tremulous. The next morning the strange dark basket was found on the steps of the seminary, but the veiled woman had disappeared. When the basket was brought to Madame Delmar and she opened it, she threw up her hands with a gasp of horror and dismay. " There's—a—a—baby in it !" she cried, in con- sternation. A note was found pinned to the baby's breast; it contained only a few words, in delicate chiro- graphy, which read as follows: "ii, tha 11A1110 of humanity do not turn this foundling from your roof. Give her the name that she must bear—Doris Brandon."

Simply these words—no more. Madame fretted and finned, but ended by keep- I lag the child. Although she grew up a singularly pretty girl, madame could never quite overcome her great prejudice against her, and she quite believed the girl was born for her especial annoy- ance—to be her especial cross. Madame was strict, grim and hard with her. She had too many tasks set for her to loiter among her companions, and her dresses were always so shabby, being made from those cast off by madame, that she was rather glad than otherwise to escape the gaze and close scrutiny of the seminary girls as much as possible. Doris never knew how it happened, but as they stood there under the waving lilac blooms, with one question leading to another, he had told this handsome stranger all of her simple history—a his- tory so dark aud so unutteiably dreary for such a fair young girl, he thought, pityingly, as he looked at pretty, shy Doris. "And when vacations roll around—then the place Is closed up, all save the western wing—it is in- deed like a prison," she sighed. "Yes, yesterday was the last day of the term ' • every one had gone, and life will be very lonely for me," and the red lips quivered. I shall be staying a few days longer in the vil- lage,' he said. "Do you think Miss Delmar would permit me to call upon you?' Doris drew back with a merry laugh. "You do not know madame," she said, laugh- ingly. "I would not dare mention your name to her, she has midi a terrible aversion to men.- She often says if she were dying, her last solemn request to me would be—thever to look with any. thing save hatred upon the face of men.' So, you see, I would not dare introduce you to her; she would foibid nie to speak to you." " You would not like that ?" asked Frederick, gen t12,-. " No," confemed Doris, hot blushes dyeing her pretty, dimpled face. " Nor ,110 Mil I. ' Le ex(.!:iiiiied, energetically. crimson dash suffused her race. lie took her handy in his. "If you will only say that you care for it, 1. will manage in some way. Do you care he per— sisted. " I—l—shouldn't mind," she faltered, shyly. "You tell me you have never had one plea— sure in all your young life," he went on earnestly. "That is so sorrowful Now, I have, a plan in my mind that would give you a great- treat." Sbe clasped her little hands together and gazed at him breathlessly. "I—could—go—to--the grand ball 7" she gasped in dismay. "Yes," he said, gaily, "but you would have to- manage it by strategy. If I were to ask madame to permit you to go, you are sure she would refuse why ask her? If grieves .ne to hear you say you: have had not one pleasure in your young life,' ' he- said kindly. "The lights and the music would be e- brilliant treat for you I would not ask you to go. if I believed there could be the least possible harm in it; it seems so cruel to deprive you of so much gaiety. If you are brave enough to slip out of the. ground and meet me here, we will go to the ball,. and I will bring you home any time you say." Let it be thoroughly understood, dear reader, that no other thought save the one desire to give this beautiful, lonely girl a bright evening happiness prompted him to make this impulsive offer. Frederick Thornton was a young man of the strictest honour. . .

He was gay and careless—a beapty-worshipperp- but no one ever yet attributed to him a dishonour- able action. He reverenced all women for the sake of his ntother and fair sisters. No broken hearts had been laid at hie door. Headers will bear this in. mind when they read what followed It was such a novel, dazzling idea—the very thought of going to the grand ball— that it quite overcame Doris with intense childish delight. "Could I get back before half-past ten, do you think ?" she asked, breathlessly. " Madame closes the house and the gates at that time." "Certainly," he reeponded, promptly; "you see, this being a village, the affair commences at nine, instead of eleven. We could be there by nine and stay until ten, and it would not take no more than ten minutes to reach the seminary, don't you see? "Yes," she answered, raising her great, childish, blue eyes to his face ; then suddenly they fell in great perplexity. "I do not know what kind of dresses young girls wear to balls," she said, with childish simplicity. "Oh, I couldn't go ; I have nothing fit to wear." "You could wear the white dress you have on," he declared ; "the best dressed young girls are always the simplest dressed and plainest." "Oh," she cried, with pretty confusion, "surely girls don't wear anything like this? "Indeed they do," he persisted; he lost sight of: the fact that the plain muslin dress, which looked very pretty in the red glow of the sunset, and with that background of bright-hued roses and green leaves, would look sadly cottrd when. brought in contrast with the sheen of satins and costly robes. The elite of the whole country would be present - at this ball. "If you are quite sure of that, and sure it wouldn't be wrong, I-1 think I would like to go—oh, ever so much," she faltered. And so. these thoughtless young people—she, s child of seventeen, and he, only a boyish young fellow of four-and-twenty—settled the matter, and they lived to rue it while their lives lasted. • At length the eventful Tuesday evening drew' near. Oh, how eagerly Doris watched for the sun to set, the dusk to creep ur?, and the golden stars to come out. Nine o'clock struck from an adjacent belfry as Doris crept out of the seminary. The coast was quite clear; there was not an obstacle to overcome. Still the girl's heart beat high with excitement; it was a terrible, almost an awful thing to do, yet she liked the excitement and enjoyed the danger, al the young and thoughtless always do. Madame Delmar, usually so grim and stern in all things, gave the girl her full freedom to roam the moonlight where fancy willed—about the picturesque grounds that surrounded the old grer stone seminary, and in this she committed a great, error. "The birds and the flowers cannot put nonsense- into he girl's romantic head," she would tell her- self, grimly. Her peace of mind would have taken flight at once, if she had but dreamed that amonf the fragrant roses there lurked a young and hallo* some man. Standing on an upper veranda in the shadow of one of the tall pillars, she saw the alight form Lit across the grounds in the moonlight. "I shall call her in presently," thought madame. Then for the next half-hour she quite forgot about Doris. In making his way toward the old south gato the thought occurred to Frederick whether or not his dark-eyed Vivian would quite approve of this little romantic escapade when she heard of it', or not. But surely she could not quite find it in her heart.because he—her lover—had stepped out, of his way to give a lonely girl a , few hours of pleasure. * He was. certainly Vivian's lover. He had nets declared himself in so many words; still, that ha& been only a question of time as to when he should. He might take a fancy to a pretty face for a cial —but his heart always went back fruithfully to Vivian. If fate had not taken Vivian away from the seminary so suddenly, there would certainly ht" been a formal engagement between them. The' diamond betrothal ring he had bought to place OP her little white hand still lay in his vest pocket. Vivian was not there to go to the ball, so wlis4 harm was there in taking poor, pretty little Doris. to whom it would be such a treat? If Vivian wets displeased about this—Vivian, who was one day to be his bride—it would be his last escapade with sol other girl, he promised himself. (To be continued).

THE INSURANCE CASE AT THE CoUNTY

COURT.

RESUMED HEA.BING AND RESULT.

_ At the Warrington County Court on Thursday, last week before his Hot our Judge Wynne Ffulke s,t)ie crie,e the London, Edinburg.h, and Glasgow Insurance Uo.,v• John Martin Dale was resumed. In this case, the Com- pany sued Dale,who was formerl C one ot their agents,for £23 19s. 101., consisting of £2O as the penalty for braking a contract with the Company by canvassing the members within six months aft: r leaving the Com- pany's employ, and of £3 19 10J., money due to the C impany. A counterclaim was aso made by Dale for £4B, as damages due for wrengfill dismissal, being the value of the defendant's bn,k, and :Mir tor various sums overcharged or overpaid to the Company. Mr. Ede mitt- son, Bilston,was also sued as guarantor. The Company was represented by Mr. G.Rhodes,barrisl-er, Manchester, instructed by Mr. Percy Marsh. solicitor, Bolton, and the defendant was represented by Mr. Foley, solicior, Bolton. Previous to the resumption of the evidence, Mr. Foley applied for the non-suiting of the Company against Hewittson, the guarantor,on the ground that he became guarantor some months after Dale entered the serviee of the Company, the former guarantor having died. After a long Iii6CUSSiOII, Mr. Foley's application was granted with costs. The evidence of the defendant was then proceeded with. He said that the names which he put down in his book,and upon which he was paid the special salary, were got by a canvasser and Mr.Seidon. When he took the book, Mr. Seddon said that he had a selling right of it, and he could get a customer for it. When he sent in I his resignation, he told Mr. Seddon lie could take the ; money he owed out of the mice of the ti,,ek. Mr Seddon said the hook was worth £35, and tor,k him to Mrs. Lea to endeavour to gat her to advance money on it, on behalf of her son, whom he, the witnes-. had nominated as his successor. Mrs. Lea, however, did not advance the money, and he thee nominat4 a young man in Winwick-road, but there was again no purchase made. Mr. Seddon then took the book, and wines.; never saw it again. It was given to Mr. S Wright —Cross exam- ined. The lapses which took place in the policies were those upon which he had received special salaries and had received commission upon. James Lea, 5, Garibaldi-St., gave corroborative evid- ence as to last witn-ss and Seddon waiting upon him, and asking him 2.35 for the book. Seddon said that the book was valued at ten times the eollectible premiums of the book, which aineunted to £4 and some odd shill- ings, but as witr.ess was a member the London, etc, (niety, Seddon said that he would let him have the beek for £35. Witness, however,did not take the book. —Mr. Rhodes : How do you rmsembar th. date that Mr. Seddon called upon you ? Witness : That is a very simple thing, because I had an addition to my family on that date. (Loud laughter.) Mrs. Lea, 43,Winwick-st., gave corroborative evidence with regard to the offer of Seddon to sell the hook. George Yeadon, Friar's Green, sa,d that he he trd Sed • don say that the book was worth £35. Mr. Rhodes then addressed the Conrt noon the evidence after which his Honoer summed up the evidence. Be said that defence had not rebutted the claim for the vari- ous sumsof 193. 51., £1 9 £16., and 5. 51. As to the agreement whereby the defender)s was pledged not to canvass the policy holders of the L E. and G.. for six months after leaving their service under a penalty of £2O, there was no doubt, whatever, that the defendant had canvassed some of them, and thorefore he must allow the claim for the £2O. With regard to the coun- terclaim for wrongful dismissal, the power of dismissal was no doubt an arbitrary right, brit he could not go be- yond the agreement, and therefore he must come to the ! conclusion that he must give judgment for the Com- pany. He would also grant an injunction retraining the defendant front canvassing any of Vie policy holders of the Society for six months commencing Nov. 23,1894. The defendant must pay costs with the exception of the order for an injunction.--Judgment was accordingly given with costa for plaintiffs on both claim and coon- rclaim.

WARRINGTON MUSICAL SOCIETY.

On Thursday evening last week the second of the two Chamber Concerts undertaken by Messrs. Crossley and Reynolds for the benefit of the Musical Society, was given in the Co-operative hail, Sankey-street, before a fashionable audience, numbering chiefly the Society's subscribers. The programme, as on the former occasion, included instrumental and vocal items of a classical character, and, therefore, more especially suited to the taste of the cultured musician. Miss Mary Hillhouse, as vocalist, was the only new name in the list of those who took I part in the concert. Her interpretation of Schuhert's "Die Junge Nonne " was, however, such 88 to at once attest her possession not only of a cultivated and powerful soprano voice,but of those more subtle qualities which are indispensible to a successful concert singer. Mililotti's " L'Esta-i in the softer and more musical Italian, and the two songs "Again my Lyre" and "My faithf a' Johnie," farther evi- denced Miss Hillhouse's ability as a vocalist,and her capacity to interpret songs widely differing in senti- ment and treatment. Beethoven's obbligati for 'cello and violin are, of course, a feature of the two last- named songs, giving, as they do, picturesque touches of tone colour as well as tuaterial support to the voice, without obtruding, as many modern accompaniments of this character often do. Amongst the instrumental items Mr. Carl Fuchs' violoncello solos were the only ones which called forth any degree of enthusiasm from the audience. The "Rol Nidrei "on the development of which Max Brach has left the distinct stamp of genius,was a masterly example of Mr. Fuch's firm yet delicate handling of his instrument, and to the varied moods iu which the recurring phrase of the Hebrew Chant is pre- sented in course of the piece, he did full justice in interpretation. The " Cradle Song " and the Ist Tarantella by the-renowned Herr Popper,were,how- ever, those in which Mr. Fuchs aroused the audience to its most marked demonstration of ap- proval, his brilliant execution of the second of these resulting in a loud demand for an additional solo. Mr. F. H. Crossley, the soeiety's conductor, played B. Tour's Romance No 2 with great depth of feeling, and altogether his rendering of this tine composition was both adequate and musiciauly. In the violin and piano sonata (Orion) with which the concert closed, the musical honours were, of course divided, but here, also Mr. Crossley'e execution was alike neat, and tasteful, The trio for strings and piano by Mr. Hudson was disappointing, and, except in the Barcarole andantino, had, in its four movements, little of breadth of treatment or charm of subject to rivet the listener's attention. Untie's trio op. 29 was only a comparative success in being more in- teresting and more pleasing. Mr. F. W. Reynolds' pianoforte solos" Nachtstiick " No. 4 (Schumann) " Lieder " No. 3 (Mendelssohn). " Album Leaf" Hider), and " Menuet " (Paderewski), were all ex- cellently performed, and attentively followed by the audience. Mr. Reynolds' ability as a piano soloist is so well-known to the Warrington music- loving public that it is needless to say more than that his execution of both the solos, the trios, and the accompaniments left nothing to be desired.

DISTRESS IN WARRINGTON.

THIL ADMINISTRATION OF RELIEF.

_ It has often been asked whether anything has been done for the poor people during the present severe weather, by any organised committee. On inquiring at the Town Hall, we were pleased to find that the local relief commitee has been at work during the past few weeks, and it+ doing good work. The relief committee is composed of the following gentlemen: Mr. F. Monks, J.P., C.C., chairman, the Mayor, Revs. W. H. Drummond, and Owen Watkins, Councillors Smethurst and Roberts, Captain }de's- ten, and Mr. J. W. Hayward, J.P. There is also a ladies' committee of which the Mayoress is presi- dent. A system of visitation has been organised. 1 and an inquiry officer (Mr. Byrne) has been ap- pointed, whose duty it is to visit cases, and make a report upon them to the committee which meets weekly. In cases of stirgent necessity, the secre- tary, Mr. Stanley Wilson, will issue tickets. We understand that at the present time upwards of 300 cases have been relieved, and that the committee are spending DO to £3O per week. The demands during the past week have considerably in- creased, and it is feared that if the frost continues, a further appeal for funds will be necessary. The balance from the distress fund last year was £283 7s. 4d. The Mayor has received a cheque of £5 from Mr. J. C. Parr towards the fund, with a promise of further donations if necessary.

'HIE EXAMINEE,

CONFERENCE A-No EE rING. On Thursds y ai tern oon and evening last week the second annual convention oi the Warrington and DiArict Association of Young People's Societies of Christian Endeavour was held in Wycliffe Congregational Church, when there was a large attendance. At three o'clock the annual conference was commenced by devotional exercises, conducted by the Rev. T. Brammall, Runcorn, and the chair was taken by the Rev. E. T. Reed (President of the Association). TV Rev. E. T. REED, in the course of an isitroductory address, said that during the past considerable progress had been made in each distiiet. The success of the Christian Endeavour movement in Warrington was due to the admirable way in which the S. cretary had carried out his duties. - I The Rev. W. KNIGHT CHAPLIN (London), having'given an address on " Christiam Endeavour 14 essentially a spiri!ual movement," the meeting was thrown open and a box was passed round to receive written questions which anyone might desire to have answered. The que,tiens were then discoursed upon, the following gentlemen taking part :—Mr. Woods, secretary of the Manchester and District Union ; Rev. 0. D. Maso (Warrington), Rev. W. Knight Chaplin (London), ann Mr. Thomas Billinge (Deacon-road Junior C.E. Society Widnes). Three very interesting and eminently practical papers were then read on Junior Christian Endeavour Work in different forms as follows :—" Its Officers," Mrs Norman (Emmanuel Jtinior C.E. Society, Warrington) ;" " Its Meetings," Miss Allatt (Newton-le-Willows Junior C.E. Society) ;" Its Members," Mr. Thomas Whinge (Widnes). Papers were read on "Christian Endeavour—au aggressive agency," by Miss Jessie Bowden (Sc. Andrewt C.E. Society, Waterloo, Liverpool), and "How can i- best contend against social evils," by Mr. H. Hope (vice president of the Liverpool and District Union). In the latter p3per Mr. Hope dealt very strongly with the sub-t ject of gambling. He said that gambling wa a prevalen in all walks of life, and he was sorry to say, as much so among young women as men. As an instance of its prevalence he told the tale of street betting in one of the main thoroughfares of Warrington which led to the dis- grace and degradation of two of the most promising young men he ever knew. The speaker knew a church which recently had a bazaar where one member boasted that he had got £5O in about 30 minutes on a draw which captivated the ladies and bled the pockets of the men. Let Christian Endeavour, he said, have nothing to do with such a thing as that. The speaker also denounced in strong terms the production and roading of filthy literature,citing examples of its extensive circulation. After the conference, a tea was held in the schoolroom fOr the convenience of the members who had come from a distance. About 250 sat down. At 6.30. the execu- tive meeting was held when the Rev. E. T. Reed was re- elected president ; Miss Allatt, vice-president ; Rev. D. W. Roberts, secretary ; Mr. A. Mounfield, treasurer. A vote of thanks was accorded to all speakers and those who had assisted in the arrangements. A resolution was passed petitioning the London and North Western and Midland Railways to offer cheap rates to Birmingham on the occasion of the annual conference. EVENING MEETING. A public meeting was held in the evening, when the chair was occupied by the Rev. John Tense, and there were also present, the Rev. E. T. Reed, D. W. Roberts, W. H. Towers (Manchester), W. Knight Chaplin (Lon- don), and J. J. Muir. F.L.S. (Liverpool). Considering the severe 'weather, there was an excellent attendance, and great attention was paid to the various speakers. IThe meeting was opened by the singing of a hymn, after which Mr. A Mounfield engaged in prayer. The Rev. J.Yonge then addressed the meeting. He said that his first word that night would be a word of hearty welcome to the members of the Christian Endeavour Society represented in that convention. Re regretted that many of their friends appeared to have kept away, for if all in the district had come, the church would not have held them. He supposed that the clerk of the weather would account for a considerable number of absentees. (Laughter.) He was sure that the fact of there being so many junior endeavourers present that night gave great hope for the future of the movement. If they could get their young people in the Sunday schools to take their part in the Christian Endeavour enterprise they might expect their Sunday schools in the future would have even more blessed results. Most of them who had been engaged in Sunday school work would have a feeling of regret that the results had not been much better than they had been. If they could persuade their young people in the Christian Endeavour Socie ties to keep true to their pledge, Sunday schools would be indeed what they were described to be,—nurseries of the church. (Applause.) The Rev. D. W. ROBERTS in his report said it was now 14 years since that grand movement had been inaugu- rated in Willaston Church, Portland, Maine, U.S.A., and such had been its success that there were now 36,000 societies with a membership of two and a half millions. Although the movement was inaugurated in Great Britain in 1887, there were in 1894, 1,989 societies mak- ing a clear increase of 1,073 upon the previous year. The first convention in Warrington was held twelve months ago, when there were ten societies who took part, with 627 members. There were now 15 societies, with a membership of 800. During the year, 40 associated had become actual mem- bers, and 111 had joined the church. The two conven- tions had helped to awaken a spiritual life amongst them, and this could not be shown by any report. The societies had done good work in many ways, and he was glad that they had six societies with a membership of 323. They were grateful for the inter-denominational fellowship which they enjoyed at such seasons as the present, and he was sure that the present convention would send them on their way rejoicing and stir them up to work in brotherhood for the one great end of sav- ing the world. Addresses were afterwards given by the Rev. W. R. Tower., of Manchester; the Rev. W. Knight Chaplin, London,the hon. see. of the British National Council,and editor of "Christian Endeavour"; and the Roy. J. J. Muir, P.L.S., president of the Liverpool and District Union. After the meeting a consecration service was held conducted by the Rev. E. T. Reed. • EDISON.

CHARACTERISTIC SCENES IN HIS EARLY LIFE. Edison's early life seems to have been almost as re- plete as Greeley's or Lincoln's with interesting inci- dents that appeal to the popular minds. About all that Edison has interested himself enough to find out concern- ing his ancestry is that they were Dutch, that his great- grandfather came from Amsterdam to New York in 1737, and that when the Revolution broke out he went to Canada. The inventor's father nartieipated in the Papineau rebellion in Canada in 1837-38, and upon its failure made a long and successful run of one hundred and eighty-two miles to the States to escape judicial death as a rebel. Edison's great-grandfather lived to he nearly 103, his grandfather lived to be over 103, his father is still living in robust health at the age of 90. and one of his aunts died at the age of 108. The in- ventor, Thomas Alva, was horn in Ohio on February 11, 1847, of a Scntch mother, At the age of fourteen he achieved a stroke of enter- prise which the newspapers have often exploited. When the news came of the battle of Pittsburg Landing he was newsboy on the Grand Trunk Riilwav, and he wheedled the telegraph operators into giving him the use of .the bulletin-hoards at the various stations for announcing the news in advance ; then he cajoled the editor of the '• Free Press" nut of one thousand of the copies of the paper to be paid for after being sold ; and finally he per- suaded the engineer of the train on which he started not to give him a few minutes' extra time at each station. The result was that every station along the route looked as if a riot was in progress ; so big was the waiting crowd that the price went up tntwonty-five gents a copy and a small fortune was garnered in. It was a short time after this that Edison came near burning up a train with his precocious scientific re- searches. He had gathered together aeon of laboratory in the car in which as newsboy he madehis trips. The car in which his experiments were carried on was destitute of springs, and, like the one.hoss shay ' of immortal memory, was in a complete and consistent state of &may. The ^Anita nt jolting dislodged the cork of a phosphorus bottle hurling it violently to the ground and lieling fire to the car. The Saws were extinguished without much difficulty, hut the wrath of the gusrel was less easily allayed. He was inclined to view Edison in the light of an umitigated nuisance rather than in that of an interesting and incipient genius. The present crowning outrage gave him the revenge his soul had thirsted for so ion", an in the twinkling of an eve our luckless hero found himself on the platform with his househol4 enolg reining about his ears. The boxing which the angry guard administered to the boa's ears hef3re jotin him resulted in the deaf- ness which has ever since afflicted him. His next feat was to construct a telegraph line from the town of P trt Huron tottus railroad station, but about the time it was completed a courageous rescue of the child of the tidegrsph operating led to Edison's installa- Von as an aPgistant in the office. Edison here started on the continuous upward career which is well known to the world, and some of the results of which are his automatic telegraph system (by which one thnn.and words a minute are sent over a wire from New Tnk to Washingtos), the electrn-moto graph (ont of which came the traesmitter of the tele- phone), the meJaphone (making audible six miles away the sound of cattle crunching grass), the aeronhone, the phonograph, the incandescent light, /cc. Here is Mr. Edison's own 9terV concerning the discovery of the principle of the phonoetaph "1 disenvered the prmeiple by the merest accident," states mr. Edison. " I was singing to the mouthpiece of a telenhone, when the vibrations of my voice sent the fine sted point into my finger. That set me to thinking. If I could record the acrinn of the point, and send the point oval. the 91frO surface afterward, I saw no reason why the thing Fhould not talk. I tried the experiment first on a strip of telegraph paper, and found that the point made an alphabet. t shouted the words " Halloo ! Halloo!' into the mouth-niece, ran the paper hack over the steel point, and heard a faint 'Halloo Rallon !' in return. I detormieed to make a machine that would work A ,ntirAtely. and Ml6' ray assistants instruction, teinov them what I had discovered. They laughed at me. That's the whole e'er's' The phonograph is the result of the pricking nf a finger."

THE PROPOSED WIDENING OF BRIDGE

STREET.

LOCAL GOVERNMEN r BOARD INQUIRY AT WARRINGTON.

On Toe: d y morning last vnek Mr. Rienzi Walton,M. lnst C. E., one of the inspectors of the Local Governnt Boai d, attended in the Council Chamber at the fowl' Hall, to inquirelinto the petition of the Mayor, atermen and burgesses of the borough who ask the Board to issue a provisional order to ernp )wer them to put in force, with reference to cir- tain lands rtquired by them for the purpose of widen- ing, opening, and otherwise improving certain streets in the borough, the powers of the Lands Clauses Acts with the respect to the purchase and taking of lands otherwise than by agreement. The proct edings com- menced at half-past ten, and amongst those present were, the Town Clerk (Mr. J. Lyon Whittle), the Borough Treasurer (Mr. J. Fairhurst), the Borough Surveyor (Mr. T.Longdin), the Medical Officer of Health (Dr. Gornall), the Mayor (Councillor James Fairclough), Aldermen Blackhurst, Francomb. Sutton, and Wright ; Councillors Bennett I Evans, R. T. Fairclough, Heaton, Hepherd, Hill, Lee' Roberts, Shaw, Smethurst ; and Messrs. Arthur Browne (representing the Warrington Property Owners' Associa- tien), F. R. Roberts, M.A., L.L B. (representing Mr. John Tott, baker, Bridge-street), John Lunt James Strange, J. Titchett„ and William Bostock and T. S. Ball, John 'nit, R. Brierley (Newton), William Owen, J. S. Garland, William Roberts, J. P. Jones, J. Bostock, and others. The Town Clerk stated that Warrington was an im- portant town with a population of about 56,000 in- habitants. The ratable value was £170,000. The sub- ject of the inquiry had been long under the consideration of the Town Council, and in 1873 the Corporation obtained a provisional order to widen Bridge-st. from Bridge-foot to Friars'-gat, but the order was not carried into effect. In 1884 the Corporation again applied to the Local Government Board for a provisional order to widen the lower part of the street, and the order had been carried into effect. The estimated cost of the im- provement was £31,107, and after the sale of surplus land £11,972. The whole of the property had been purchased for £30,993 The net cost alter the sale of iurpliia land was £16,550, which was, unfortunately, £4,578 above the estimated cost. That was made up by a loan obtained from the Local Government Board of £16.800. and of which they took £16,550. Since the lower part of the street had been widened the widening ot the upper part had become imperative. Bridge-st. was a most important thoroughfare, and was the main road between Lancashire arid Cheshire. The Borough Surveyor, in answer to the Inspector, said the width of the street that had been widened was 50ft., including the footpaths. The width of the foot- paths varied. On the weA side they were 10ft., and on the east side 13ft. and 141 t. Continuing, the Town Clerk said that for nearly 30 miles the only vehicular bridge of communication ' between the two counties was Warrington Bridge, and practically up Bridge-st. In 1834 they had not a single hackney carriage or 'bus licensed for hire ;now they had , 66 licensed hackney carriages and 'buses, with an equal number of drivers. 'Buses frequently travelled up and down the street. The resolution with reference to the widening of the street was unanimously car- ried by the Council. All the statutory re- qoirements had been complied with. The pro- perty owners had been approached, and there was a probability of coming to terms with some of them. One shop in the block proposed already belonged to the Corporation. There were eleven houses of the labouring class in the district, but there were plenty of houses in the town of a class similar to _those it was rroposed to pull down. There were 31 occupants :altogether. Eight of the cottages had been reported upon by the Medical Ofiller as unfit for numan habitation. Six of the eight cottiges were occupied. Toe Borough Surveyor said it was proposed to make a new street from Friars'-gate to Rylands-street, to get the back portion of the land into the market for build- ing purposes. The length proposed to be widened in Bridge-street was 213 lineal feet. The total area of land to be acquired was 4,652 square yards. and the land to be given no for street purposes 1,920 square yards, leaving 2,732 square yards for re-sale. Ha considered the total cost of purchasing the property -marked on the entire area, if they were bound to proceed by arbitration (it was quite anticipated that many of the properties I would be purchased without, which would reduce the cost very materiall7) at £31,189, and for re-sale, old ' materials, &e., £B,23s,leaviug a total cost of £22.954. The Inspector asked if anyone present wished to question the Borough Surveyor. Mr. Arthur Br )wne, solicitor, said he represented "The Warrington Property Owners' Asscciation," which consisted of more than 100 members. The assoeiatien was increasing daily, and represented 2,000 rated houses. Replying to the Inspector, the Town Clerk said that there were 10,535 inhabited houses in the borough. Mr. Browne asked Mr. Longdin if it was not a fact that between 500 or 600 houses were empty in War- rington at the present time. The Surveyor said he was not son of the fact. There was no doebt a large number empty. Mr. F. R. Roberta, solicitor, on behalf of Mr. Toft, baker ane confectioner, of Bridge Street, asked Mr. Longdin how tar back the Corporation intended to set the building line, and was informed that the distance would be 50 feet. The Inspector (to Mr. Roberts).—Your client's property is to be taken up altogether. The inquiry shortly afterwards terminated.

PRESENTATION TO A BLIND MEMBER.

In the old Masonic Room, Sankey Street, on Wednes- day last week a meeting of the memt era of the above 0-der was convened in order to meet the Grand Secret- ary and Grand Treasurer of the Order of Druids Friendly Society who C,lme to present the award of the Accident Compensation Fund to Brother George Ogden of the Mermaid Lodge, he having lost his sight by paralysis of the eyes caused by his employment as a fiatman. The award was granted by the Board of Management. 'rhere was a good attendance of members of the War- ington district branch, and the chair was taken by Samuel Dimbobbin, D G.M. who was supported by Grand Secretary Brother John Westall and Grand Treasurer. Brother Godfrey Fligharn, and officers of the Warrington district. In a brief speech the Chairman commented in feeling terms upon the sad calamity that had fall-m upon Brother Orden who had lost his sight while still a young man of 36, with a wife and family to support. Brother 02den had been 16 years in the Society and though he had only paid one penny a year for sixteen years to the Accident Compensation Fund, be would now receive in return the handsome sum of £4O —Brother G. Fligharn mentioned that this was the first time the sum of £4O had been awarded from the fund, 120 having hitherto been the amount. He then presented the amount to Brother Ogden who feelingly acknowledged the donation, and the grand secretary, Brother Natal!, then gave an interesting address. The meeting was brought to a close with votes of thanks to the visitors.

LITTLE SANKEY PARISH COUNCIL _ THE EXTENSION OF THE BOROUGH.

DEFINITE DECISION. • meeting of the Li tie Sankey Parish Council was held at the vestry of the Wesleyan Schoolroom, on Thursday evening. The chair was occupied by Mr. R. B. Mat- thews, and there were also present, Messrs. W. Brough. r. Greenwell, S. Williamson, J. Corradine, F. Massey, B. Grimsha w, Jackson, G. Thompson (district Council- lor for the township), and T. Tumitail (clerk). It was stated that the cost of the recent election was £2s,and it was resolved to levy a penny rate,which is ex- pected to produce £3O. A letter was read from the Town Clerk of Warrington, in which he submitted to the Parish Council the proposals of the Corp-iration with regard to the inclusion of the township of Little Sankey in the proposed extended borough. The Cor- poration offered the ratepayers the differential scale of rates for 15 years as follows :—a, for 5 years after the date of incorporation the ratepayers will he exempt from payment of the improvement rate ; b, for a fur- ther period of five years th e ratepayers are to be exempt from lof the improvement rate ; e. for a still further period of five years the ratepayers will he exempt from of the improvement rate. It was considered that these terms were advantageous to the townskip,as they would lead to a reduction of the present rates for 5 years. Further, the Corporation will pave the the footpath on the south side of the Liverpool-road within six months after the incorporation and the foot- path on the north side within two years of the same. They also undertake to pave Gatewharf-street as far as the railway crossing and the footpath at St. Luke's Church will also be made good. It has not been theught desirable to increase the number of Town Councillors and therefore, it will be impossible to give the district a separate councillor and the township will be joined to St. Austin's ward which will then have 1134 burgesses. The township will also be properly patrolled by the borough police and the inhabitants will in other ways have all the advantages of the present burgesses of the Borough. They will have a free library and a free fire brigade ; the price of gas will he reeuceri and also that of water ; burial fees will be lowered one half and the Corporation will endeavour to provide a free play ground. After a long discussion it waa decided on the motion of Mr. Corra.fline, seconded by Mr. Greenwell, to accept the offer of the Corporation subject to the ap- proval of the parish meeting which would be called on Thursday. February, 28. h. This was all the bipiness.

The latest ent stir eit under the "New Woman's " harmer is Mrs. E M ,ke, of 22, Union-street. Wells, , Somerset. She ogei..:e empires the new with the old and what it really means, and it throws a new light on the subject. If this is what is meant by the "New Woman," no husband can object, for not only is her happiness enhanced, but his eomfort, for what is more conducive to home comfort ;hen a cheerful, enereetic, contented, healthy wife? She writes: " Nine weary months of ilLness redeeed me to a skeleton. For twenty years I had been subject to slight attake of drecoy and weakness of the heart, wh oh in February, 1891, culmin- ated in a serious permenent athae.k of dropsy. INDEPENDENT LABOUR PARTY.

THE PROBLEM OF LIFE. MEETING AT EARLESTOWN.

A largely attended meeting under the auspices of the Eirlestown branch of the Independent Labour party, was held in the Co-operative Hall, Earlestown, on Mon- day evening. Mr. William Gee,of Warrington, prodded and there were present on the platform Mr. James Sexton (Labour C)rreepondent to the Board of Trade) and Labour Candidate for lehton-under-Lyne, Me-are. Schofield, Swarebrick, Gray, France, Roughley, Wright, Miller and Fairolough,—The Chairman said he was pleased to see so many present and if they were not all ' socialists there was some possibility ef m +king people socialists who took the trouble to come, particularly if they were open to conviction. Mr. J. Sexton said his subject, "The Problem of Life," might require some explanation of what was meant. Thee' problem of life " was the problem which at present faced most of them, the reason why Gal's creatures men, women, and children were not allowed to live a life of decency and of comfort, in the richest cities, and the richest empires of the world, not from the partisan point of view, bigoted point of view of Independent Labour, nor of Tory or Liberal, but of a human being recognising the right of every human be- ing to live out hie life to a natural and logical conclu- sion. (Hear; hear.) Having gone thus tar, he would examine what claim man, woman, or child had to live, and the basic upon which he 13 ised his argument was the argument of the enemy of Socialism, the argument that was advanced from every pulpit in the land, the argument of the political economist who based his politi- cal economy on Grices Word,and he was content to build the basis of his Socialism on God's Word. If they took the first chapter of the Bible, going no further than Genesis, they found that in the beginning of the world God made man ; he placed him on the earth and said, "All these things I give unto you, the birds of the air, the fishes of the sea are thine for ever, go thou, there- fore, be fruitful and multiply." Suppose they took stock? Where were they to-day ? The multiplication was all right so far as they were concerned. There were, a good many of them there that day, more than there were when Adam and Eve entered the Garden of Eden. andithe birds of the air,the beasts of the field, and ihe fishes of the sea had gone on and multiplied in propor- tion, but what about the division ? Did it nut strike them that there was something wrong there. Take the land. Three elements were necessary for man's exist- ence, land, pure air, and food, and enough of it. Those three elements were indispensable to men, women, and children living their life. He would take the and. There were 72 million acres of land in Great Britain, and there were 39 millions of people, mostly Liberals and Tories. (Laughter.) The 72 million acres given to man by God for his use for ever were owned by less than half of one million, the other 28 millions being by them shut out. He had heard it said by workingmen politicians that they could live without the land. One of the most necessary elements far man's existence was the land, which was now monopolised by a very small minority of the peeple. The question of pure air was essentially necessary for the existence of human beings. Had they got good air? He slid not know how they were situated in Earlestown, but they did not get pure air in the Liverpool slums •, they did not get pure air in I the factories, or in the chemical works, because the I basis upon which society rested at present neeessitated that man should be shut out from the land in the first 'instance, so that somebody else might have both land land pure air who do not produce anything. Then there I was the question of food. Did they get enough food, if they did how came it that a million and a half men, representing three times as many human beings, had not sufficient fool wherewith to live from one day to another. The argument of their enemy was " you Socialists do not understand the political economy of the day,which rests itself upon the good, sound, commercial law of supply and demand." There was the natural law of supply and demand, which was the only one they were willing to recognise. On the one hand there was a natural demand for food of nearly four millien human beings, and on the other hand there was a commercial supply . . .

wherewith to keep these human beings in existence. What kept the two elements from coining together P That was the question they wanted to ask themselves very seriously. (A Voice : ' House of bords.") No,it was not the House of Lords ; that was only a very small item in the matter. The House of Commons had more 1 to do with the question than the House of Lords. The ; greatest sinner was the House of Commons, which ' represented the capitalist class of this ceuntryil A Voice :(" we are not having that"). He was not coming there to denounce either the House of Lords or House of Commons, nor capitalist, because they were all creatures of circumstances and of environment. The duty of I.L.P. men waste be sure of their ground,before they struck out, and then they could launch e.ut 89 much as they liked. If the law of supply and demand cut one way, it would surely cut another.--"rake the factory system of to day. They were coming nearer and nearer every day to a climax in the textile industries they were sending away from this country, a large a- mount of machinery to China, India, America, and other places to he placed in factories to enable them to turn out products which used to be supplied by this country. The wages in the textile trades were going down, the fac- tories were bound to shut up, and if they were not able to find work in other directions fur those interested in textile work, he was afraid there was goiug to In a big row. The million and a half of starving people knocking at the doers of the legislative chamber would grow to seven millions. Twenty millions of land only, under cultivation in England out

of a possible 72 milli-ms—was not that an eloquent fact that the men were driven from land to compete with their brethren in the factories, in the coal pits, in the dock yards, ind other places. The proper utilisation ot the land would find work For three times the number who were starving to-day. Why was the land not utilised ? The natural conclusion they arrived at was that the men who were responsible for the present system, found it totheir interest to keep it as it was,so that they could al- ways have more than enough men to do the necessary amount of work required to be done. He was a trade unienist,and he believed in trade unionism. He believed It was a gool thing, and had &lee a lot in the past 50 years, hut if one third of the 90 million pounds that had been spent by trades unionists in 50 rears, had been spent in sending trades unir nists to Parliament. to Di. trict Councils, and Boards of Guardians,they would have been in a better pnaltinn to-day than they were, from an industrial point of view. Their plea for the nationa- lisation of machinery was equal to that for the nationa- lisation of the land. They could very well do without the capitalist. lie could pack up as soon as be liked, becalm when he had gone the land would still be there,

and from the land spra—ng all the necessary elements oi capital. Wealth was the product ot labour, am' withou, lablur there could be no wealth. The policy of socialism' was, " that he doeth no work, neither shall he eat, the man who does work shall be assured a life of decency and comfort." The men who did all the work at present , produced all things, did not live a life of decency and comfortmil the men who did not produce them lived a life of luxury and splendour. (near, hear.). Several questions were put at the close of the address, asked by members of the audience, chiefly bearing on the House of Lords, and the payment of members. Mr. Gilmour said if there was a general election, he wanted the I.L.P to fight either Liberal or Conserva- tive. and not tight indepsndentle when there were tWC) candidates in the Anil. The difficulty would be in ruin- ing a third man.—W. Sexton said the Labour party had a right to nnmlnate a man even if there were seven can- didates in the Ther had the right, and they were going to exercise it in spi'e of Liberal or Tory. The meeting shortly afterwards terminated.
