"ST. PAUL IN ROME."

A lecture in connection with the Y.P.S.C.E. of Bold. ' at. Wesleyan Church, was given upon the above subject ' on Monday evening, by the Rev. G. D. Mason, in the B iid-st. Wesleyan sch oolroom. There was a fair &Used- a nce. The lecture, which was a very interesting one, was iilestreted by limelight views, the lantern manipu- lator being Mr. D. A. Stone. In the course of his lecture, Mr. Marion said that he did not think it worth while to trouble his hearers with references to the vari- ous authorities upon the subject, and he would proceed at once with his remarks. Amid all the dim and waver- ing traditinns concerning other apostles and their con- nec' ion with Rome, one figure stood out distinct end undeutited. It was tee figure of St. Paul. No criticism or scepticism of modern times had ever questioned the perfect autnent ieity re that last chapter of the Acts of tne Ap steles which gave this account of his journey, stage by stage till he set font within the walls of the city. The apostles would enter Rome by the Appian Way, the oldest, and most renowned of all the wonder- ful roads whereos the Romans bound the ends of the earth to the eternal city. For miles the Appian Way was one long avenue ef death. Burial in the city was for- bidden and it teerefore became the custom of the power- ful families of R one to bury their dead by the side of the great roads. Mr. Meson then went on to describe the various places of inrerest to be met with along the i Appian Way, and also the caraconabs. The apostle would niter Rens by the Porte Capene and would then be delivered up to the Putinun Guards, where he would ' be handed over to the proper authorities to be dealt with. After his case had been formally reported, he was given as large a measure of liberty as was possible to -ice whose appeal to the emperor had yet to be heard. Such appeals were slow processes. It might be years before the case was tried. There are cnly traditions sites of the epistle's dwelling place to be seen now. In al probability,SePaul took lOgings with some Jew,perhaps with Aquite and Priscilla or perhaps Pudens. It mignt be taken for granted that his lodging was not far from the Jewish settlement, which was the famous Ghetto, and derived from a Hebrew word meaning cast off, or abandoned. In the time of St. Paul, the Jews of R esei were a most important and powerful colony. Under their friend and pritetitor Julius Crew, they had in- creased with iticredible repidity, and their influence quickly became felt in the palace of the emperer itself. They were, however, always held by the Romans as objects of intense abh irrence and disdain. It. is generally believed that about the year 64 AD., St. Paul.% was brought up for 'll4 and was acquitted and liherated For two years more he was free to prosecute his mis- sionary lateens, and seems to have travelled once more to the east if not to Spun. Very soon after he ha l left Rome there occurred that disastrous ere, the blame of which Nam cast up in the Christians, and which led to their first awful prosecution. It was impossible that the founder of the church should escape, and orders were sent to Asia ti it Paul should be arrested and brought to Rome. Us seems to have been arrested at Troy. He WAS sent to Ephesus for trial, and once mere he appealed unto Car. This time, however, Nero had determined te make short work of Christians, and it was probable that but little time woule elapse before his mockery ef a trial, in a court already determined oa his death Ha Roman citizenship would serve him how- ever for the usual terrors which accompanied capital punishment in Remit, and his sentence was that he alieuld be beheaded. On the day of execution the Apostle was taken out of his cell by tho guard. Fis nearly three miler; beyond the gates the sad proctaion walked along the busy read between Rome and her p irt of Ostic. Littls did the gnarl think that they were ! marching in a proeessioa more really triumphant than any they had ever followed in the train of general or • emperor along the Sacred Way. For that prisoner was marching along a nobler Sacred War to a noble Capital. His captivity at, tat Was over. The heroic soul was soon to drop the citaie of that feeble body. Paul the snobs'- stint had at length fulfi led his long mission. Re had now arrived at the palace of his King, he had come unto Mount Zion, unto the city of the living God, to the heavenly Jerusalem, and to the innumerable company r of angels. The word of the executioner fleshed for one moment, and the spirit of Paul made perfect wet for ! ever with the Lord.

DICKENS' " CHRISTMAS CAROL."

RECUAL AT THE CO-OPERATIVE HALL..

Though not large, there was a fair and fashionable at- tendance at the Co-operative Hall, Sankey-at., on Mon- day evening, when Mr. John Harwood, of Blanchester, gave an admirable recital of Dickens" "Christmas Carol " far the benefit of the Cairo-st. Sehool Extension. The chair was taken by the Mai or (Councillor James Fair- dough), who after introducing Mr. Harwood, retired amongst the audience. The evening's programme was divided into four leaves or chapters arranged as followi: —Stave I. : Marie, 's Ghost appears to old Scrooge us Christmas Eve, and warns him as to the future in store unless he changes his mode of living. Stavell. : " The Ghost of Christmas Pest " ; The shadows of the thing. that have been ; Scrooge's Childhood ; Old Fezaiwig's Christmas Ball ; The Parting. Stave " The Gliest of Christmas Po sent " ; Beb Cratchit'e Christmas Dinner ; Poor " Tiny Tim " ; The Party at Scrooge's Nephew'e and the game of " thindmati's Buff" ; The two little children," Ignorance " and " Want." Stave IV. : " Ihe Ghost of Christmas yet to !come " ; The Merchants o- 'Change ; Old Jee arid the Ragshop ; Death of "Tiny Tim "; The neglected Grave, aad Scrooge's Appeal to the Ghost : The Awakening and Transformation. Dickens' o'Chri,tmae Carol "is too well known and too much admired to need any explanation, hut still in jus- tice to the elocutionary skill evinced by the reciter, some comment is necessary. To commence with Mr. n irwand clearly showed by the wonderful inflections of voice he was capable °tend also the manner in which he could transform the ape pearance of his factures for the further enhancement of his interpretation of each cearacter, that elocution was net the limit of his powers. In the, first stave his imitation of Scrooge's cross-grained surliness. was only exceeded by that of the t eme person's newly awak-n rid terror at th. appearance. in ehoetty array, of his seven years' defunct patner, Jecoh Marley, whose impressive warning changes Scrooges jocose incredulity into tern r and dismay. Etch 'nc dent in Stave 2 was brought nut with vivid reality by the reciter who had now cap, ured and afterwards retained the full interest of his audience. Scrooge, as represented by hina was thoroughly compatihle with his r,haracter as conceived by Do:kens, and the hearer eag rly followed the development of termer' feeling in Scrooge's heart, as ' startled into existence by the visions of the past laid before him.—Childhond's recollections were turning hi. Mind into new chennels. Stave 3 brought Mt Har Need's elocutionary powers into tullr play and his imperoonee tion of the varied characters assembled at Bob Cratchit's Christmas dinner called forth smiles and applauee,being , almost sufficient to persuade the hearer thet e.ch indivi- I dual represented was a personal aquaintance. The joy and merriment present with the diners &need itself am .eg the audience who were, however, fully appreciative of Scrooge's asides, as he fast became penitent. Save, 4 was the most impressive of ell.and when after able e l- ing 'with the first visit of Scrooge and the those e turns to The Ragsnop and Old Joe, a burst of an, hie., greeted the reciter's interpretation of the diffi chw - actor of the latter. This was indeed Mr. Harwoodes triumph,—the bent fieure, trembling hands, quavering voice, and dry chuckle of Old Joe were pourtrayed life. Tiny Tim's death was produced with all its touch- ing pathos fully revealed and intensified, but the grand finals came with Scrooge's appeal to the ghost over ote neglected grave. Here is his final and complete repent- ance. the coming which is witnessed throughout the recital. The.swalritning and transformation with which the recital cleiees, formed-a fitting and happy finish to an evening's undoubted enjorment. Before leaving the room the Rev. W. H. Drummond, BA., expressed the indebtedness of the audience to Mr Harwood for his recital and to the Mayor and Mayoress ' for having graced the gathering with their presence. He thought those who had listened to Mr. Harwood that night would hope it would not be lone before he again c ame to Warrington. (Hear, hear,and applause.) The Mayer respending, expressed his appreciation of the recital.

TOYS AND• GAMES.

The Largest Selection of Toys in the SCity. Prices from Is. to Eh. Rocking Horses. Dolls, Mail Carts, Swings. Pole Horses, Bassinettes, ke. All gnaranteed English make. ALL THE LATEST PARLOUR GAMES is. EACH. SLADE, 73, BOLD-STREET. LIVERPOOL.

Tun, Opposition appear to have Parliament. already given up all hopes of an early defeat of the Government. The first critical division was taken in the House of Commons on Friday evening last week on an amendment to the Address and the Government secured a majority of twelve. This is probably the closest division there will be for some time, as the Government were deprived of their ordinary majority by the abstention of a few of their sup- porters. The debate on the amendment was in no way notable except for the fact that it clearly indicated that, in the opinion of Tory politicians, the one distressed class in • the country, is that of the landlords. The troubles and embarrassments of this class formed the staple of nearly all the speeches that were made by members of the Oppo- sition, and the debate culminated in a declaration by Mr. BALBOtrit which has been very generally construed as meaning that Protection might kayo to be resorted to in order to keep up the rents of the landed gentry.. The real unemployed—those who are suffering not from a reduction in their rent-rolls but from a deprivation of the actual means of subsistence—were scarcely ever mentioned, and this in spite of the fact that the amendment had, been so framed by Mr. 13Au1etra that it prevented another member of the House of COLIIMODS from directly raising the question of 1 unemployment. This is further proof, if further proof were needed, that Toryism is still in the main synonymous with landlordism, and that if it is again returned to power its main object will be the aggrandisement of an already privi- leged class.. .

Another amendment to the Address in which a great deal of interest was taken was moved on Tuesday evening by Mr. J. REDMOND the leader of the littl& band of Parnellites. It called upon. the Government to dissolve Parliament and to submit the question of Home Rule to the con- stituences. Mr. RBDMOND professes to be acting on the lines laid down by Mr. PARNBLL, but in that he is undoubtedly mistaken. It was certaialy Mr. FAiniss&L's policy in the early days of his Parliamentary career to "block the way"with Home Rule, but after the Liberals had undertaken to give self-government to Irelargl and to give it the foiemost place in their programme his tactics changed, He then acted in concert with and not in opposition to the Liberal party. It was his object, just as it was that of Liberals, that the party should be strengthened in every possible way before an appeal was made to the constituencies. He knew, as everyone must know who has given five minutes consideration to the subject, that it would need the utmost force of the Liberal party to secure the passing of a Home Rule Bill. Mr. Raemono seems to lose sight of this .circumstance, and in- stead of strengthening the party which is pledged to Home Rule, he is doing his best to weaken it at a very critical period. As Archbishop CROKE, in a letter to the "Freeman's Journal," very forcibly puts it, Mr. Itimmoxo and hi % friends are trying to "discredit and depose" the friendly Govern- ment that has led Irishmen to the very confines of Home Rule because it cannot achieve impossi- bilities, while they seek to instal in power the "hereditary revilers of their religion and race." Fortunately, Mr. REDMOND'S amendment, although it was supported by the whole force of the Unionist party, was defeated by twenty votes. The Parnellites, will, we should think, find some difficulty in justifying their action in Parliament to their Irish constituents.

GOOD. TEMPLARS CONVENTION.

THE latest news of the war in the The War in the East. East shows that the Japanese have inflicted another serious blow upon their " Celestial "antagonists. They have captured,after a severe bombardment and several more or less sanguinary skirmishes and assaults both by sea and land,the strongly fortified place of Wei-Hai-Wei, together with the remnants of the Chinese fleet. In these latest enc3unters the Chinese " braves " have shown a little more courage than they did in most of their previous engagements, but perhaps this is to be accounted for by the fact that there was no line of retreat open to them. They were between the Japanese and the deep sea, and under these circumstances they seem to have fired their last shot before hoisting the white flag. It is 'now evident that the Chinese government will be reluctantly com- pelled to acknowledge d ofeat and to pay the re quired indemnity. It has no reliable reserves to fall back upon for its defence, and it is therefore almost certain that the Japanese will soon be thundering at the gates of Pekin The Chinese have long been in need of a few modern ideas. They have been under the sway of the most unadulterated form of Conser- vatism, and through not having the advantages of any Radical or progressive element in their politi- cal life they have fallen far behind the po Werfill nations with which they are brought more and more into contact. Their utter inability to cope with the Japanese, whom they have aflected to despise, must surely have enlightened them cow sTderably, on military matters at all events.

I

cording to a letter from a gentleman resident China, which has been published in one of the London papers this week, Chinese knowledge 0'

the military art is amusingly elementary. We Sre told that the military mandarins have formed sox° magnificent schemes for comfounding the enernY One of these schemes was for the destruction °f the enemy's fleet. Two determined men wore t° be employed, one of whom was to be a good swim' mer and diver, and when the Japanese fleet anchored off the coast one man was to engage the enemy in conversation while the other was to quietlY slip into the water,fix a torpedo to the propellor of one ship after another, explode it, and then escape by swimming! Another scheme was to fig a junk with dynamite, cover the explosives over with vegetables, and when the vessel was in the midst of the Japanese ships, a devoted hero 00 board was to apply the match and blow himself and the whole fleet to smithereens. The reward for this self-sacrifice was to be a family Po' sion and the ennobling of ancestors to three generations. Yet a third plan of the resourceful MOLT/LES who control the C:tinese army was t° arm each soldier with a bag of pepper wherewith the enemy was to be blinded and then helplesslY slaughtered. It will be generally admitted that there is no hope, from a military point of view,fer a nation which expects to combat modern warlike weapons with bags of pepper.

THII report which Major Meals" The Chelford DIN has made to the Board Of Accident. Trade with regard to the Chelfora railway disaster is not a sensational document' It acquits the stationmaster who had charge 0f the shunting operations which led to the accident of any culpable negligence, but at the same time it points out methods by which similar accident might be prevented in the fature. The accidellt was, it seems, a preventible one, and if tile' stationmaster had realised that the force of wind was sufficient to move a wagon it might have been prevented.. The terrible loss of life if 4. not attributed to any defects in the rolling stock of the train,- as the carriages were all strong, and of comparatively modern build' The one tangiblo result from the collision and the subsequent inquiry is a dation to rail; way companies to lay down in their book ef rules and regulations that during high winds ell wagons are to be kept attached to the engin° when being set back to their trains; or, if thig would cause unreasonable delay, all lootN3 wagol3s are to have the brake pinned down as soon 93' they come to a stand. To make this operatic° safe and easy wagons should befitted with a brake- handle on both sides instead of only on olio. eid° which is the rule at present.

RUNCORN CEMETERY. From the monthly statement of Mr. W.F. Salkeld, the secretary of the Runcorn Burial Board, it •appears that the interments for the ten months ending January 25th' numbered 257 as compared With 452 for the corresponding period last year, Showing a decrease of 195. The fees received amounted to £249 55., as against £420 Bs.Bd. during a similar period last year, or a decrease of £l7l as. Bd, in fees.

A RUNCORN SAILOR DROWNED. The body of John Roberts, whom we reported to be missing in our last issue, was found off the shore of Beaumaris, on Saturday. The deceased resided at Queen-5t., Runcorn,and was mate of the steamer Swift, belonging to 'Messrs. Brundrit and Co., of It..,uticoto• He had been missing since the night of the 30th ult., and it is supposed that he fell over- board when returning to his ship in a small boat at Beaunaaris.. An inquest on the body was held at that town on Tuesday. ACCIDENT TO A RUNCORN BOILERMAN. He WaS Mployed as a boilerman at Mathieson's Chemi- 'etial Works, Widnes, and was in the discharge of his A ties, when a valve, owing to expansion of steam, bli toi 2`tst, and Griffiths received serious injuries to his ' iree,as well as a compound fracture to his right leg. e Was removed to the Accident Hospital, where he Was attended to by Dr. Hutchinson. S.&MINS INSTITUTE, RUNCORN. There was a crowded attendance at the weekly entertainment provided at this institute on Monday evening. Mr. A. Abel presided. The Mersey Mis- sion Band performed several selections. BREAKING THE ICE.—ACCIDENT ON A STEAM

===== 0002601_18950216_art0051.txt | included=true | included =====
TUG.

Amongst the tugs being used to keep an open waterway through the ice 14 the Ship Canal is the steam tug Pioneer, and on Wednesday afternoon, whilst it was thus engaged near Runcorn, a some- what unusual accident happened. Owing to the jerking of the machineryin the boat's endeavour to make headway through the mass of ice, the neck of the safety valve was broken. The steam rushed out, and scalded the engineer about the face and arms. He was medically attended to, and is now progressing favourably. BRUNSWICK-STREET MUTUAL IMPROVEMENT

SOCIETY.

Mr. A. Higgins, of Liverpool, read a very inter- esting paper before the members of this society on Wednesday evening. His subject was "Dungan, the good of things as they are." The paper was really a defence of Socialism, lie considered that the pulpit was to-day out of touch with the times, and it was one of the things that ought to be re- formed. The paper was criticised by Mr. J. D. ' Jones (chairman), Messrs, William Massey, T. Leach, and S. Coventry. ANOTHER SUCCESS OF A RUNCORN STUDENT.

At the examinations held recently in connection with the London Victoria College of Music, at the Liverpool Centre, Miss Warburton, daughter of Mr. I. E. Warburton, passed with honours, in the inter- mediate grade, both in piaioforte playing and in theory, she having obtained the highest number ot marks of any student examined. Mr. J. H. Lewis, Mu. Doc. D.C.L. was the examiner, and Miss Warburton was prepared for the examination by Mr. S. Wadsworth, F.C.C.G. Liverpool.

ICH AC'CIDRNT AT RUNCORN.

On Tuesday afternoon, whilst two boys, respec- tively named Henry and George Blight, of 47, Shaw-st., were playing on the icebound Bridge- water Canal near to Savage's Bridge at Runcorn, the younger brother, Henry, fell through a hole in the ice. The little fellow went overhead ani had it not been for the prompt action of his brother, who went into the water and rescued him, he would most probably have been drowned. The two boys were taken home, and with the exception of a wetting, were *not much the worse for their immersion.

FUNERAL OF AN ODDFELLOW AT RUNCORN.

The mortal remains of Mr. John Owen, of Bold- at,, Runcorn, were interred in Runcorn Cemetery, on Tuesday afternoon. The deceased, who was one of the founders of the Duke of Devonshire Lodge of Oddfellows,No. 5,055,was 59 years of age. Amongst these present at the funeral were Joseph Slade, H. B. Toone, G. Rutter, and G. Riley; P.G. W. E. Rutter (secretary), Robert Wright (treasurer Past Grand Lodge), and Bros.S. Johnson, E. Onions, and W. Hayegr. The Rev. J. Simpson, Congregational minister, conducted the funeral service.

AB9EMBLY BILL IT RLINCDRK.

There was a large company 4t, the annual assembly ball held in the Public Hill,Runcorn, on Wednesday evening. The room had been most tastefully decorated for the occasion by Mr. James Steele. The programme included N dances, six of which were shadow dances. Messrs. Lea and Son provided the limelight apparatus. Mr. Merchant. of Chester, supplied the music. The refreshments were supplied by Mr. A. Riley, Bridge Street, and Mr. George Shone. Dr. McKenzie and Mr. Shone acted as M.C's and were ably assisted by Mr. Clement Handley and Mr. G. Pritchard. Dancing was kept up with zest until the early hours of next morning. INDRPENDENT ORDER. OF RECHABITFS.

On Wednesday the annual meeting of the Run- corn and Frodsliam district was held in the Duke of Westminster Cocoa Rooms, Runcorn. Mr. W. Cartwright, D.C., presided, and there were also present representatives from all the tents in the district. The annual financial report showed that the district has been in existence about seven years, and the total funds which are available for funeral claims only amount to .053 6s. 10d., being an increase of £5l 178. 6,1. on the year, and a total membership of adults and juveniles of 481, which was considered very satistantory. The result of the election of officers was as follows :—Mr. Joseph Booth (Runcorn). D.C.R. ; Mr. William Lockett (Frodsham), D.D.R. ; Mr. W. CAMDEN WEBLZYAN CHURCH.

The members of the Camden Wesleyan Church choir, with the church offidals, and a few friends, sat down to tea in the schoolroom on Tuesday even- ing, at the invitation of Mr. Speakman. The Rev. T. Brackenbury occupie4 the chair at the subse- quent proceedings, and delivered an excellent address on "Church Music." ' Mr. S. C. Beckett, who presided during a portion of the proceedings, spoke of the importance of regular attendance at church, and also referred to the value of good music at the services, alluding to the help it gave to minister and congregation. Mr. Speakman urged the choir to do the best they could. He expressed a fear that, they had not paid that attention which Ithey ought to have done in the past. He had in- vited them with a view to recognising and en- couraging them in their efforts. lie advocated the rendering of an anthem each Sundsy evening. Mr. A. Harrison and Mr. Joseph Bagley and others also spoke. A programme of vocal and instrumental music was afterwards submitted, and greatly ap- preciated. TRH COUNTY COUNCIL ELECTIONS AT RUNCORN' The two retiring members for Runcorn on th e Cheshire County Cauncil are Mr. Philip Speakma J.P., and Major Harlehurst. The latter gentleman has already signified his intention of retiring from the representation of the North' division of the town, stating that he is unable to give the time and attentian ' which that honourable !position re - quires. Mr. P. Speakman will again offer his ser- vices to the electors of the South division, and there is every likelihood that he will be re-elected without a contest. He has been a most useful mem- ber of the Council, and his attendance at the com- mittee meetings and Council meetings will compare favourably with those of any of the other members. Far the vacant seat, Mr. S. Taylor, J.P., has been , asked by an influential body of electors to become I a candidate, and he has acceded to this request. No other candidate has yet been put forward, and Mr. Taylor will probably be returned unopposed. The election addresses of both candidates will be found in our advertising columns' Mr. J.. Ashton lis the returning officer, and he has fixed five. o'clock on the 22nd inst., as the latest time for the handing in of nominations. He will attend the following day to hear Any objections. THE REV. J. LILE-STONE.

This Wesleyan minister, who for a short time has been stationed in the Runcorn circuit, has, on the advice of his medical attendants, intimated to the Superintendent (Rev. T. Brackenbury) that he will be unable to undertake ministerial work in ae circuit after the present quarter. Much regret has been expressed, amongst Wesleyans in the district at this information, and at the continued indis- position of the rev. gentleman. He was becoming a most popular preacher in that circuit and had made a large circle of friends. CONCERT AT WHITLEY.

On Thursday evening, a grand concert was given in the Church schoolroom, Whitley, the proceeds of which were in aid of the Church repairs fund. The programme was provided by the following Runcorn artistes: Miss Taylor (soprano), Miss Simkin (con- tralto), Mr. Niz.kson (bass), Mr. Mason (coster), and Mr. A. Grounds (humorist). The accompanist was Mr. W. Grounds, organist of the church, who also contributed a solo on the pianoforte. A dance suc- ceeded the concert. A SKATING FATALITY.

The Cheshire police have ascertained that the two young men who were drowned while skating on the Dee on Tuesday were James Howard, of Roberts-street, Widnes, and Frederick Leatbwood, of: Warrington-road, Alton-view, Widnes. They left home on Tuesday morning for a day's skating on the Dee, and when they had proceeded about a mile up the river they left their coats at the White House. The bodies cannot be recovered before the ice breaks up. Thousands of people were again skating on the The on Wednesday.—Both bodies were recovered on Thursday, and have been identified as belonging to Widnes, as mentioned above.

BURNING FATALITY AT WESTON POINT. CONFLICTING EVIDENCE. THE GRAN rING OF CERTIFICATES.

At the Dock Hotel, Weston Point, on Thursday morn- ing, an inquest was held by Mr. J. E. Worsley ,coroner, relative to the death of a child of Mr.Meakin,lo,Mersey- view, Weston Point. Louiia Ann Meakin, mother of the child, was the first witness called, and stated that her husband was a salt boiler. The deceased child was 4i years of age. On Wednesday or Thursday, the 9th or 19th of January last.about eleven o'clock,the deceased was in the kitchen alone. Witness was going through the back kitchen when she heard screams and on looking she saw that the deceased had fallen into the fire. He was partly dressed at the time. He was burnt on the right shoulder, but there was only a small scar. SllO put some vaseline on it, and on the following Monday her husband took the child to Mr. Griffiths, herbalist, Alvanley. The wound then seemed to go on nicely until Friday last when it was worse. She sent for Dr.McKenzie, of Runcorn,who saw the child on Sunday night. It died about three o'clock on Monday morning. Dr. McKenzie said he found the child suffering from a very severe burn over the right shoulder joint, and it was actually dying when he got there. No properly qualified medical man had seen the child since it had been burnt. If the child had lived the joint would nave been useless, as the muscles were completely burned away. He could see into the shoulder joint. It was a moat serious case. It would have been better for the child had the medical man been called in earlier, but he did not think there had been any wilful neglect. Tne child must have been leaning against the fire bars for some time to have caused such a serious burn. _ .

_ Rebecca Stephensen said she saw the child after the accident, but the injury did not then seem serious. She had been told that morning that on the date of the ac- cident the child had been thrown on the fire, but she did not believe it. _

Mrs. A. Foster, 13, Clarke's-terrace, said she lived opposite to Meakins, but sue did not hear of the burn- ing accident, until after the child had died. She had heard the husband and wife quarrelling. A few weeks ago he ran his wife out of the house, and she stayed away about two days. Eva Meakin, a child about seven of age, said she remembered her brother getting burnt. Ile was sittinz on her daddy's knee when he fell off into the fire. He was picked up by Gertrude, her sister. Mrs. Whitby, South-road, Weston Point, said Mrs. Meakin was her mother-in-law. She saw the child after It was burnt, and Mrs. Meakin told her that the deceased had fallen. She admitted that there had been some quarrelling between husband and wife. The burn did not look very serious. Mr. Meakin was fond of the child, and she did not think he would do it any harm. Mr. Meakin was then called into the room, and told that if he gave any evidence it would be taken down in writing and might possibly be used against him. He said he did not wish to give any evidence.—The Coroner : The evidence already given is that the child fell off your knee into the fire.—Mr. Meakin said that that was on another occasion altogether. He was not in the house at the time the child was burnt. His wife told him of the accident when he came home to dinner, but he had not then time to look at it. He took the child a few days afterwards to a herbalist,and he gave him some ointment. Be ultimately sent for the doctor. He admitted that the child did fall eff his knee on one occasion, but that was before Christmas. The deceased then caught its head on a shovel, making a scar on the brow. In answer to the Coroner he said he did have a quarrel with his wife a few weeks ago, when the chil- dren ran out of the house. But that was not at the time the child was burnt. He knew nothing as to how the child received the burns.

Mrs. Meakin on being recalled said the child Eva was at school at the time of the accident.

Mrs. Whitley was also recalled and said she was in Meakin's house when the child fell off Mr. Meakin's knee and caused a scar on its head. That occurred be- fore Christmas. He had a " drop of drink" at the time. Time child Eva was not present on that occasion. The Coroner in summing up said that in this case a certificate had been given of death by the doctor and also by the registrar under a misapprehension which would be necessary for him to explain. Wherever there had been a death due to accident or a death due to vio- lence, although other causeamight supervene which might be actually the cause of death, still an inquest was always necessary, and information given to the police before the certificate was issued or before the registrar granted a ceitificate. The jury returned a v-rdict of " Accidental Death."

WESTON • POINT.

BRAVERY OP A CARAT OFFICIAL.—Ori Saturday morn- ing, John Hughes, of Runcorn, whilst engaged in the dredging department of the Ship Canal Company was descending a ladder near to the Weston and Mersey Lock, when he slipped and fell into the waterway. The accident was seen by Mr. Murphy, lock-keeper, who with promptitude entered the canal and rescued the man. When brought to the bank it was found that Hughes had sustained injury to his hip. A cab was pro- cured in which he was conveyed to his home. It may be added that Mr. Murphy has previously displayed Intrepidity in this neighbourhood, and that during the following of his occupation here and elsewhere he has been instrumental in saving 16 lives. _ .

ThIA haa thrown about 40 men out of work, andis entirely due to the frost as there area good number of orders in.

AN AMUSING SEWING COMPETITION.

A few days ago the spinsters of the Hotel Petersburg, at St. Moritz, in the Engadine, invited the gentlemen whom they favoured to tea and a sewing competition. Each competitor was provided with a square piece of red calico (which he was expected td hem) and other re- quisites, also a spinster, who directed his movements and instructed his ignorance. The faces of some of the men were a sight to see ; they wore the agonised look peculiar to bicyclists intent on beating a record, and skaters trying to learn the outside edge backwards. When time was called the sewing ceased, and the speci- mens were handed to a committee of matrons for adjudi- cation. The that prize, " Views of the Engadine,' was awarded to Dr. Chidell, who well deserved it. Neverthe- less, as doctors learn stitching professionally they can- not be regarded as amateurs, and should be handicapped accordingly. The second prig., a sliding book holder, was gained by Mr. Elliot Crompton, who acquired the sartorial art during his wanderings in the wilds of Africa, where, in the absence of civilised womankind, he had to mend his own clothes—so long as he possessed any. The Consolation Stakes, a squeaking frog in trousers, went to Mr. William Westall, the novelist, who was held to have attained the bad pre-eminence of being the worst seams- tee in the Petersburg, probably in St. Mori•z. Mr. Westall is the author of a novel entitled "As a Man Sows." De can nnw write another and call it '• How a Man Should Sew."

RUNCORN COUNTY COURT.

DISPUTE ABOUT THE PACKING OF CHINA CLAY.

At Runcorn County Court on Tuesday, before His Honour Judge Wynne Ffoulkes and a jury. Peter Kil- cross, of Shaw-st.. Runcorn cooper, claimed £2O from S. W. Royse and Co., merchants, Albert-square, Man- chester, for casks and for packing 40 tons of china clay. Defendants admitted their indebtedness, but alleged that the material was negligently packed and in dirty casks, and they claimed £29 14s. 9d. damages, being the difference between the sum they received from it and the sum they would have received if it had been in proper condition. The case was originally entered in the Runcorn Court, but was removed to the High Court, to enable the detendants to take evidence on commis- sion by letter, and it was again remitted to Runcorn, by writ of certiorari. Mr. Greer, barrister, Liverpool (in- structed by Mr. Burton, Runcorn) represented the plain- tiff, and Mr. Langdon, barrister, Manchester (instructed by Messrs. Needham, Parkinson, Slack, and Needham, Manchester) appeared on behalf of the defendants. Mr. Langdon said tlaare was no dispute whatever about the plaintiff's claim. The defendants admitted that Mr. Kilcross was entitled to the amount charged. Messrs. Royce and Co., however, claimed damages from I Mr. Kilcross, for negligence in packing china clay in I December 1893, or January 1894. The defendants were anxious to send out to Boston a consignment of china clay, which being the first shipment, they desired to be packed with special care so that English „roods might compete with the American Markets. They therefore wrote" Please see that the casks are good and strong, and that the clay is in perfect order and condition and there is no dirt and impurity. Indeed we rely on your giving yoor attention to this lot." This agreement was entered into ; but instead of the clay being packed in clean casks they contained something impure and injured the clay, so that before it had been in the market a week Messrs. Roye received complaints stating that when the clay was dissolved it showed black spots, evi- dently charcoal. A very long correspondence followed between them and the American house, with the result that it had to he sold for £l7 Bs. 2d. instead of £46 103. Even if his client received the amount claimed it would be small consolation, because the American Market had been closed to them, as the people would not buy their clay. The question was were the black spots caused by the pasking ? They had samples of the clay sent, which Was without a single impurity, but the one returned from America contained defects, which 'ere evidently of a vegetable nature. Mr.; W. Barlow. 32, Mayfield-road, Whalley Range, Manchester. in the employ of the defendants Royse, chemical and general merchants, Manchester, said he had the management of the matter in dispute. In De- cember 1893 there was a quantity of china clay at Run- corn which had been brought there from Cornwall in the "Agnes Cairns." Witness communicated with Mr. Meadowcroft, the local agent of the Manchester Ship Canal, at Runcorn, respecting the shipment of some of it to Boston, America. He left the matter of packing ha the hands of Mr. Meadowcroft. In January, 1894, Messrs. Linder and Meyer, their agents at Boston, ack- nowledged the receipt of the china clay, In a later com- munication they reported unfavourably of the clay, stating that they found it " gritty." When dissolved in water it showed black specks which looked like charcoal. They tried a number of casks and found them all the same. The packages were very poor, they being second- hand ca!ks. A sample was sent to theirs from America and on dissolving it, witness found that a number of black specks floated on the top. The sample which was sent to them from Runcorn did not shaw the same de- fect.

William Mottram, stock-keeper, in the employ of the Manchester Ship Canal Company at Runcorn, gave evi- dence as to drawing a sample of the china clay, which was sent to the defendants. He took the sample from the Insides of two lumps of clay. He did not submit any of the small dust. In cross-examination he admitted that the sample was a fair one. Mr. Barlow then dissolved a portion of the two samples in Court, the one from Ameriea and the other from Runcorn,and these were handed to the jury. Con- tinuing his evidence, he said that Kilcross told him that he had packed the clay in second-hand bleach casks, but he stated that they had been scrubbed before using. John Meadowcroft, manager of the Ship Canal at Runcorn, stated that an order was given to Kilcross to pack the clay.—By Mr. Greer : He could not say how long the clay had been in the sheds et Runcorn. He received a consignment note from the railway company stating that the casks in question had been shipped in good condition. Mr. Langdon then read evidence which had been taken by commission in accordance with agreement. On behalf of the plaintiff, Mr. Greer contended: that the clay was put in proper casks, and efficient men were employed. The complaint was that the black specks had arisen through the negligent conduct of plaintiff in getting dirty second-hand casks, entirely unfit. for the purpose of packing fine clay. They were obtained from Messrs. Mannion, of Manchester, and were such as those generally used for packing clay. Mr. Kilcross assisted in the packing of the china clay in question. He did all that a careful workman could. Referring to the first letter received from America, he stated that they ac- knowledged that the packages were good. The speaker also pointed out that the clay arrived in a coasting vessel, and might have been in contact with Coal; also that it was handled by the employes of the Ship Canal Company; and that it was subsequently shipped at Liverpool. It was impossible for the casks to.cause the black specks which were in the centre of the clay. Peter Kilcross deposed to receiving the order to pack the china clay, and to doing the work in a workmanlike manner. He examined the casks and saw that they were properly clean. They were the same he had been using for 15 years. He used similar casks for Leemoor clay, which was the finest clay. He had not had a single complaint from those who had given him orders. William Joseph Mannion, 57 Albany-road, Chorlton, said his father carried on the business of a coop,r, at Manchester. Witness was the manager, and he remem- bered receiving the order for casks from Mr. Kitcross. He saw that they were clean, and the casks supplied were solely used for the packing of this clay. Robert Harrop, 64 Lock-street, Runcorn, and Peter Ganley, Fryer-street, Runcorn, gave evidence as to fill- ing the casks with clay. They saw that the casks were perfectly clean. His Honour summed up the case at length, and the jury after a short consultation returned a verdict in favour of the plaintiff, and disallowed the counter- claim.

THE SEVERE WEATHER AT RUNCORN.

As might be expected the effects of the continued frost are being felt at Runcorn. The shipping trade at the port has almost been brought to a standstill. On the Bridgewater Canal, instead of the usual barges plying along with their cargoes, there is to be seen a host of young persons sliding and skating. The river Mersey has been rendered unnavigable from Garston upwarth, caused by the floes of ice. The Ship Canal has, with great difficulty, been kept open, owing to a similar rea- son large masses of ice having found an entrance by the tidal sluices. Runcorn, in fact, in the words of a correspondent " is icebound In every direction. Its pits and its canals, quite apart from the Manchester Ship Canal, are absolutely impassable, flats and crafts of various kinds being as fast embedded in the ice as the proverbial frog inside a piece of rock. Business is entirely suspended in the ordinary waterways, and the canal population has given itself up to winter merri- ment, if net to resignation. Most of the fl sating popu- lation has, in fact, gone in for skating, so that from Runcorn to Warrington all sorts and conditions of men and women, as well as children, have girt up their loins and taken to scudding along the numerous stretches of frozen water to be found in that neighbourhood. The country far and near reminds one sf a Russian scene, only there is more exhilaration between Runcorn and Warrington, than there probably is over the whole of Russia. The construction of the Ship Canal has broken into old waterways in various places, and the water being still locked in, with no possibility now of the out- or of tidal effect, the skating community has acquired some of the quietest and most reliable happy "skimming grounds." It was on Saturday morning when the Ship Canal be- came impassable at Runcorn owing to the tremendous influx of huge blocks of ice. The Ls. Grampus, of Glas- gow, was thy first to suffer, it becoming embedded in the ice, so much so that some of the crew ventured to land on terra _firma whilst the ship was in the centre of the canal! Other smaller vessels were placed in the same unfortu- nate position. The official account supplied by the Ship Canal Company of the blocking of the Canal is as fvl- levist—" The officials of the Manchester Ship Canal Company experienced very little difficulty in keeping the Ship Canal open for navigation until Saturdav,wheis the rising tide brought enormous masses of floe ice, which amounted to some thousands of tons, from the estuary of the river Mersey, through the tidal openings into the Ship Canal near Ruacorn. Large quantities of this ice accumulated immediately above Old Quay swing bridge, Runcorn, with the result that the as, Grampus on her passage from Manchester to Glasgow was unable to pass through it. The Ship Canal Com- pany's steam hoppers, Alpha and Beta, were available for breaking ice in the Canal, and they attempted to relieve the Grampus, but were unable to get through the ice pack, and the company's powerful four propeller dredger Manchester. which was working on the tipper section of the Canal, was requisitioned. The Mulches- cheater succeeoed in forcing a passage through the pack, and after proceeding down the Canal to the Weaver sluices, returned to Old Qii.ty. The s.s. Grampus left Old Quay on Sunday, and proceeded towards Eastham. It will be remetnhereo that the tidal epenings referred I are the„ the e,estruction of which was put upon the Canal Canpany by the opponents of that undertaking %Chi, n in Parliament." It may be stated that Mr. Marshall Stevens and Mr. Hunter superintended the arrangements. On Monday, and during the week.the Manchester and other dredgers have been engaged in keeping the waterway navigable, and in this they have been successful. Owing to the frost, the men employed by the Ship Canal Co., at the Sprinch works, have been put on half-time.

STRANGE DEATH OF A CHILD AT HA.TTON.

THE INQUEST: SINGULAR EVIDENCE. A death presenting some rather .suspicious circum- stances took place at Hatton last week. It appears that a single young woman named Ada Maddock,about 23 years of age, recently returned to the village from a home in which she•had been employed as a domestic servant. Her mother perceived that she was enciente, and wae, of course, very much grieved to find her in such a condition. Cu Sunday last week the young woman was confined of a female child, but as no one was in the heuse at the time the event ' was kept for several days as a secret. llowever, on Thursday Mrs. Meddock's suspiciens were arouged owing to certain events, and she questioned her daughter, who admitted that she had given birth to a child, that it was dead, and that it would be found in the back bedroom. A search for the body was made, and it was found. By the coroner's instructions a post-mortem examina- tion of the body was made cm Saturday by Dr. Bowers, who found that the child, which was fully developed, bad breathed. The police were represented at the inquest by Superintendent Atherton, of Runcorn and Sergeant Gosling, of Stockton Heath. The foreman of the jury was Mr. James Parr, sem The girl Maddock was unable to be present. The first witness called was Mary Mad- dock, who stated that she was a single woman living at Hatton. She was a " farm labourer." Ada Maddock the mother of the child, was her daughter. She came to her house some two months ago,and witness noticed that she was then enciente. She had lived with witness since. Witness did not know until her daughter told her that the confinement had taken place. Witness went with her to see the child. She did nothing to it. She simply looked at it and covered it with a cloth. The next day she wee to see the doctor, who paid a visit to the house on Friday night. Dr. H. E. Bowers, practising at Stretton, said that on Friday, the Bth February, he went to the house and found her daughter, Ada Maddock, sitting up, but com- plaining of giddiness. She said she had made no pre- paration for the coufinement, which had occurred on Sunday afternoon, as she did not want her mother to know. Oa examining the body, witness found that it was that of a full-grown female child. He requested the girl's mother, Mary Maddock, to give information to the police, and he did so himself on the following day. The umbilical cord had been torn MI at the placenta end. It was coiled twice round the neck of the child and once round the body. 'There was also a bruise at the back of the child's head. On removing the scalp there was an extravasation of blood beneath correspond- ing with the external bruise. Thera was no fracture of the skull, and the brain was normal. On placing the lungs with the heart attached in water they fhated and buoyed up the heart. When placed and squeezed in water they also floated, which showed that the child had breathed, but owing to the card having been coiled round the neck of the child, strangulation might have taken place before the .complete separation from the mother, as under the circumstances the pressure round the neck must have been great.—Cross-examined by the Coroner: He did not consider that any importance was to be attached to the bruise on the head. There was no fracture. In his opinion the cause of death was strangu- lation by the umbilical cord during birth for 'want' of proper attention. If there had been a second person present the child would have been released. Mary Maddock was re-examined, and she said that her daughter had a child about 18 months ago. She was then attended by Dr. Bowers, who did so at the request of the relieving officer of the Runcorn Union.. .

The Coroner summe7l up the evidence, and remarked that the concealment which had taken place had given cause to very great suspicion, and it was necessary that a strict investigation should be made of all the facts. Had the child been made away with the case would have assumed a very serious aspect, but the evidence of the doctor was clear, and he thought that the verdict of the jury would be " Death from strangulation by the umbilical cord, due to want of attention at birth, the mother being delivered alone and unable to give the necessary attention to her child." The jury returned a verdict accordingly. . '

In answer to Superintendent Athertori,-the doctor said that if the mother continued to improve as she was doing at the present moment she would be able on Mon- day next to leave her home.

WA RRIN GION.

LECTURE ON TANNING.

On Monday evening the second of the lectures on tanning by Mr. J. Gordon Parker, Ph.D., F.C.S., was delivered in the lecture-room at the Museum. Many of the employers and employes engaged in the tanning trade were present. Councillor Shaw Green presided. At the close of the lecture, the Chairman asked those present for an expression of opinion as to whether it would be desirable to form systematic classes for the study of tanning in con- nection with the Technical Institute next winter. A favourable opinion was expressed by Councillor William Pierpoint and Mr. Charles Parker. The last-named gentleman explained that a • similar class to the one proposed was at present being carried on in Penketh. The opinion ot those pre- sent was then taken, and it was unanimously decided in favour of the class. A vote of thanks to ' the lecturer brought the proceedings to a close. STOVES.—GAnLsten, Heating Engineer, Warrington. DEATH OF THE REV. WILLIAM COTHAM,

It is with regret that we announce the death of this highly respected clergyman, which occurred at Stonyhurst College, on Saturcluy. He was the eldest son of the late Dr. Cotbam. formerly of Hardshaw Hall. He left Warrington for Stony- burst in 1819; and after going through the pre- scribed course he entered the Society of Jesus, of which order he was the senior priest at the time of has death. He frequently visited his friends at Warrington. He was engaged in missionary work between 50 and 60 years. He was at Ugbrook (in Devonshire), Wigan, Preston, Liverpool, and St. Helens. He was for some years at Prescot, where with the aid of his confrers and friends, he built the stone church and supplied it with bell, organ and other requisite furniture. At the time of Ms death he was in his 89th year.

WORKINGMEN'S SOCIAL EVENING Under the auspices of thy Gospel Temperance Mission,Buttermarket-st., an enjoyable social enter- tainment was held on Saturday night in the hall, Mr. S. Stringer presiding. Mrs. G. Ayers sang with much feeling "On the Cross of Calvary" and "God gave His Son for me," and Mr. J. Houltram 1" The Anchor's Weighed." The remainder of the proceedings was of a social character. On Sunday night a gospel temperance meeting was held. Mr. J. W. Tanner. who presided, spoke of the blessings of temperance and Christianity. Mr. ti. Taylor, police court missionary, based his remarks on Jus- tice Denman's views of the evils of the liquor traffic, but differed from his views on the question of moderation. Mr. Riley contended that it was only just and right that the people should have the veto of the liquor traffic. It was evident that all who opposed " local veto " were friends of the drink traffic, and were afraid to entrust the people with liberty to control the traffic. . .

MANCHESTER.

At a meeting convened by the Lord Mayor of I Manchester of riparian authorities in the counties of Lancashire and Cheshire which was held at the Town Hall, Manchester, on Tuesday, all the authorities, with the exception of Widnes, were represented. Alderman Sutton, Councillor Roberts, the Town Clerk (Mr. .1. Lyon Whittle), and the Borough Surveyor (Mr. T. Longdin) were present on behalf of the Corporation of Warrington The representatives of Salford, Warrington, the Warrington Distric!, Council and Lymm District Council expressed their approval of the scheme, and their willingness to join the proposed authority, while, on the other hand, Widnes and most other Rural and District Councils objected to do so mainly on the ground of expense. Ultimately a resolution was passed that in the opinion of the conference, it was desirable that a port sanitary authority (which includes the Ship Canal and the Mersey from Ince to Hunt's Bank in Manchester) be constituted for Man- chester, and that the Local Government Board be requested to hold a local inquiry on the subject. OUR LOCAL CHURCHES-WARRINGTON.

WYCLIFFE P.S.A

The attendance, on Sunday owing largely to the severe weather, was not quite so numerous as usual, but those who attended were amply rewarded by the specially high quality of the whole meeting. Absentees missed a treat, as they usually do. The chair was taken by Mr. G. Bayley who spoke upon the importance of making true use of these meetings, as opportunities for considering earnestly the bent and bias of ,our life. His few words were well and earnestly given. The chief speaker was Mr. Henry Webb, J.P., of Bury, who spoke with marked ability and power. He took as the basis of his address the word" Climb." Be said that it was a word only used twice in the Bible, one instance being in Joel 2. 7. 'They shall rut like mighty men, they shall climb the wall like men of war.' He said that lie supposed none of his hearers were either soldiers or sailors,but they were men, and meant to mount up in life and character. Taking the word "Climb " letter by letter, he spoke on climbing courageously, climbing lightheartedly, climbing intelligently, climbing man- fully, and climbing belieringly. His address was full of telling points and he was accorded long and hearty applause. Mr. Hipkiss delighted the audience with a solo on the clarionett '•Cujus Animam," accompanied on the organ by Mr. W. H. Payton ; Miss Booth sang very sweetly. "Take the name of Jesus with you." The lesson was read by Mr. Harvey Evans, and prayer offered by Mr. T. Hawthorn. It was announced that next Sunday would be the " Ciaen "Sunday, when the members bring their wives. Mr. Chandos Wilson, of Manchester, will speak. CAIRO-ST. CHAPEL.

The Rev. W. H. Drummond continued bis course of addresses on the history of Unitarianinn at the Cairo- St. Chapel, on Sunday morning.dealing with the history of the movement in England. In the evening Mr. Dru- mmond delivered the second of the advertised course on "Religion in Tennyson's ' Idylls of the King,'" speaking of the poem entitled "Gareth and Lynette," an outline of which he gave, and then pointed out the lessons in- culcated. The service was rendered additionally attrac- tive by Mr. Watts rendering very efficiently as a solo, "Comfort ye," and "Every valley shall be exalted," accompanied on the organ by Mr. J. M. Muir.

BOLD:STREEr iVESI,EYAN CIIURCR.

The usual people's service w-a;-helaTnThe—Bald- street Wesleyan Church on Sunday afternoon. There was a large attendance, and the meeting was conducted by the Rev. J. Kirtian. The address was given by Mr. J. R. Allen, of Lymm. De said the question of pre-eminent importance to-day was, " What might be done to better the condition of the people r After referring to the various sug- gestions which had been made, and the methods which had been adopted to this end, he said that the remedy for all the woes of suffering humanity would be found in none of these things. They must come to Christ; and when in every day life there was a practical application of His teaching, then would come the day of which poets had ever sung, and to which prophets had ever looked for- ward. Jesus Christ had said " Learn of Me, for I am meek and lowly in heart; and ye shall find rest unto your souls." If they obeyed his commands, and emulated His examples, their homes would be better, and their lives would be brighter. He pro- ceeded to show that the temperament would be in- fluenced by taking Jesus Christ as their example. The more closely they followed His teaching, the nobler, purer. and more Christianlike would be their charaeter. The highest type of life was that of the Lord Jesus Christ, and the highest ambition they could possibly have was to live a life similar to His. Miss Jean feelingly sang" The Better Land," and Mr. Allen gave a violin solo which was thoroughly appreciated, as were also the selections performed by the orchestral bend.

THE DISTRESS AT WAR- RINGTON.

ICE CARNIVAL IN AID OF THE UNEMPLOYED.

An ice carnival in aid of the unemployed took place on Monday evening on the splendid sheet of ice attached to Twiggery Farm, near the Cemetery, which Mr. James Newton generously placed at the disposal of a Corn- , mittee. The ice was illuminated by several " Wells" lights,and although the committee had only a few hours' notice a good evening's entertainment was put forward and thoroughly enjoyed by the large number of specta- tors present. The chief event of the evening was a skat- ing handicap for ladies, 120 yards, in which 24 competi- tors took part for the valuable prizes offered, and after some excellent racing the following ladies were success- ful in securing prizes :-Ist prize, Miss E. Grimshaw, 20 yards start, a ladies' companion presented by Alderman Wright; 2.1 d prize, Miss G. Ratcliffe, 25 yards start, ladies' work box presented by Mr. Joseph Allison; 3rd prize, Miss M. Coop, scratch, ladies' satchel, presented by Mrs. M. Whittle, Marquis of Granby; 4th prize, Miss L. Allen, 10 yards start, case of spoons, presented by: Messrs. Skelton and Comp toy. After the above event a race was arranged for the brush brigade there being 7 competitors and Messrs. G. Melyneaux, W. Molyneaux, and F. Maloney securing Ist. 2nd. and 3rd. prizes res., pactively. During the evening, Messrs. Lockett and Sherwood gave an exhibition of ornamental skating which was most interesting to the spectators. Mr. Alder- man Wright (who had given mast able assistance during the afternoon) in a neat speech presented the prizes and thanked Mr. Newton for his generosity in granting the use of the ice,and also Mr. Habberley and his co-workers for the admirable manner in which the Carnival had been conducted. The following gentlemen took charge of the proceedings : Chairnsan of Committee, Alderman Wright ; hon. secretary,Mr. W. Habberley ; Committee, Councillor Shaw, Messrs. Longdio, J. Farrington, J. D. Brimelow, H. Hope, J. T. Whittle, W. Wood, J. Willett, E. Taylor, W. Haigh, Joseph Allison, and F. Donley. There will, we understand, be something like £2O to hand over to the relief fund. MEETING OF RELIEF COMMITTEE.

A meeting of the executive committee which was ' elected last year to take charge of the fund remaining' from the subscriptions which ware received to relieve the distress sonsequent upon the coal atrike of last year, was held in the Town Hall on Thursday morning. The com- mittee has already been doing good work, and the secretary (Mr. Stanley Vi ilson) reported that up to that day, 1,396 tickets for food had been issued at a cost of £157 is.. and in addition to this 1,296 coal tickets at a cost of £43 4s„ and that the total surn expended was £222, leaving a balance in hand of £77 Bs. 4d. The committee resolved to open a soup kitchen on Friday, and through the kind permission of the volunteer officers, the Drill Hall was appointed as the place for distrihutinn. The hours of distribution were fixed from 11 o'clock to 12.30 p.m., and the method was on similar lines to those adopted during the coal strike, tickets to be obtained at Bank House. It was also decided forth- with to make an appeal for subscriptions, through the local press, and to continue the issue of tickets for coal, but no further tickets for food were to be issued. The 'Master Builders'Association have contributed £2O to the fund, and have issued tickets to men directly engaged in the building trade, through Mr. R. W. Collin of Marson-st. The committee will meet every Thursday.

IMPERIAL PARLIAMENT. (continued from nage 6.) _ THURSDAY.

In the House of Commons on 'Thursday evening, Mr. AsqulTH informed Mr. WOODS that he proposed intro- ducing at an early opportunity a bill deAling with the question of explosions of coal-dust in mines. Mr. ACIAND, replying to Mr. COURTNEY, said he would I instruct inspectors to judge leniently schools whose effi- ciency had been temporarily impaired in consequence of irregular attendance caused by the inclement weather. In answer to a queAtlon, Mr. BRYCE said he proposed to introduce the bill for providing light railways at the earliest opportunity he could find.

Mr. MORLEY staied that Dr. WALSH, the Roman Catho- lic Archbishop of Dublin,. and Lord PLuxaerr, the Prostestant Archbishop of Dublin, had been appointed to fill vacancies on the Board of Commissioners of Irish National Education.

Sir W. Ileaconar, questioned by Mr. BANBURY', said he was informed by the National Debt Commissioners that the securities held by them on account of the de. posits of the Post-office S ngs'fiank had hitherto earned more than sufficient to pay the present rate of interest. But the margin was growing extremely narrow, and he proposed to appoint a departmental committee to inves- tigate the subject. The debate on the Irish amnesty amendment to the motion for an Addr,,ss in reply to the QUEEN'S speech was resumed. Sir W. HARCOURT said the Government regarded the amendMent as a censure upon them, and he appealed to the Irish members to allow the House to come to a vote. Mr. SEXTON said he did not regard this las a vote of confidence, but if the Government did not act upon the logical ronsequernes of their Home Rule Bill and grant an amnesty they would be surrendering the exercise of the prerogative of mercy into thc hands of the House of Lords. Dr. WALLACE, Mr. ILLlNO- wortra, and Mr. LOGAN supported the amendment, and Mr. MATTITEWS opposed it. On a division the amend- ment was rejected by 299 to 111. .

Mr. A. MORTON mpved an amendment regretting that the reselution of the house of May 5, 1893, In regard to the appointment of county magistrates had not been fully carried out. Sir W. HARCOURT said the resolution of the House had beed carried out so far as was possible. The LWID CrIANCRLLoa was desirous that there should be no di3enfranchisement for creed or class, and in that spirit be had appointed a number of working- class men with great advantage to the administration of justice. The amendment Was withdrawn. Mr. HARRnsaTom moved an amendment in favour of an inquiry into the r se of the men in prison for the murder of the JOTCA family at Maatnstrasna, county Galway, in 1832. Mr. MoncEr promised to give his at- tention to any new facts which might be furnished tn him, and the amendment was withdrawn.

MESSRS. BRUNNER, MOND AND CO., LIMITED. THE HALF-YEARLY BALANCE SHEET. A 30 PER CENT. DIVIDEND.

Appended is the report of the directors of Messrs. Brunner, Mond and Co., Limited, Winnington, North- wich, to be submitted to the half-yearly meeting of the shareholders at Liverpool on Thursday next : The balance sheet and profit and loss account, certi- fied by the auditors, and now presented, show a balance to the credit of profit and loss Recount on the working of the half-year ended on the 31st December, 1894, of £145,075 4s. 2d., which, with the amount of £89,592 8. 10d. brought forward from the previous half-year, imakes a total of £234,667 13R. I The directors propose to deal with the above balance I as follows :—.

Dividend on the preference capital, at 7 percent. per annum Dividend on the ordinary capital, at 30 per cent, per annum Amount to be written off patents account 2.500 0 0 Balance to be carried forward

Total

1,667 13 0

It will he noticed that the debt owing by the holders of the 22,500 ordinary shares issued in 1884, for the premium of £lO per share, has during the last half-year been paid off, and the item finally disappears from the balance sheet. The shareholders are requested to note that the divi- dend warrants will be posted on Tuesday, the 26Ph inst. The directors who retire in rotation are Mr. Milner, Mr. bland, Mr. Schellhaas, and Mr. Crosfield, all of whom are eligible for re-election. The directors recommend to the shareholders that Mr. Alfred Moritz Mond, barrister-at-law (am of Mr. Lud- wig. Mond), be elected a director. The audibers retire, but are eligible for re-election. THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, FEBRUARY 16, 1895.

M-s-rs J. GAdes and J. H. Fleming.

Teounts STORE.—James Weedon was charged with being drunk and disorderly in Winwick-st., at 12.30 a.m. on Wednesday morning. P.C. Evans in proving the case said that defendant was committing the offence outside the Central Station, and that he had travelled from Manchester to Liverpool without t,ickt, and the railway officials were going to take proceed ngs against him.—Prisoner admitted being drunk and said, making a soldierly salute to the Bench, "I am most happy to say I was never in a Police Court before." No doubt this assertion had some effect, for he was let if with a fine of ss. including costs.

And costs. ATTIEMPTIM SUICID/I.—John Dickenson was charged with attempting to commit suicide at Golhorne, on January 21, by planing himself across the rails of the Imndon and North-WArtern railway. The prisoner had been remanded from the Warrington Petty Session s at which place it was stated that prisoner got on he railway at Goiborne and seeing a train coming, he placed his head on the rails. Two men who were on the bridge above saw him, and shouted to him. He thereupon raised him,elf to o,lt at them, and the train going past he escaped unhurt. —Superintendent Brassington said that the man had been drinking, hut he now promised to return to work.—Mr. Taylor, P.C.M., said that he would find the prisoner some work to do.—The pri- soner's brother who was in court promised to look after him, and he was accordingly discharged. A JUDICIAL SRPARATION —George Cooper, Newton, was charged with assaulting and beating his wife Miry Cooper, on January 28. A separa- Con order was also applied for.—Mr. lit. Stuart, who representori the wife, s iid that the parties had been married 18 years, and had several children. During the greater portion of her married lie, the complainant had had to maintain herself and her family, and she and her husband had not lived happily together. Matters cul- minated on January 26, when 'llooper kick d his wife violently. He was then drunk, and he wai in the habit of ill-treating his wife every time he was drunk, which was very frequently.—The complainant who was called corriihnrated her solicitor's statement, and said that she was in danger of her life. Her husband could earn 255. pr week. The magistrates considered that there was ample j.er ifieation for a, separation. FOOTBALL.

ASSOCIATION.

HOLY TRINITY V. BRICK-ST. MISSION, WARRINGTON.— This match was played on the Holy Trinity ground in cold weathe,... Howard started for the visiturs,and play ruled pretty even, until Seddon got possession,and mak- ing a smart run down ese field, sent in a shot which just grazed the crossbar. The home front rank again made tracks for the visitors' goal, Seddon finishing up with a neat shot, which just missed scoring again. After two attacks had been made on the Trinity goal Rigby cleared, and Pugh coming down with a rush centred finely. Haddock heading through. The hou3esters played up with more determinatiou,and after several unsuccess- ful attacks, Seddon, from a quick shot,notched a second goal for Holy Trinity. A slew game ensued for a time ; the homesters were awarded a free kick in a favourable position,Clegg scoring the third. Half-thne arrived with Trinity leading by three goals to nil. Resuming, the Mission brightened up considerably, and Lezge, from a free kick, shot into the goal mouth, Rigby failing to clear let it in. Smart passing by the Trinity backs enabled Seddon to have another shv, which came to nought, the ball going just outside. One or two corners fell to the Mission, but they proved fruitless, and they had to retire defeat( d by three goals to nil.

APPLICATION FOR TEMPORARY AUTHORITY.

THE CHIEF CONSTABLE AND THE APPLICANT. UNUSUAL HESI CATION. On Monday morning, at the Borough Police Court, Mr. A. Browne, solicitor, applied for temporary autho- rity to sell at the Queen's Hotel, the property of the Corporation, on behalf of Stephen Stirrup, and in lieu of James Burrows. In answer to the question formally put to the Chief Constable—if he had any objection to , the application, Mr. Hunt (chief constable) showed un- usual hesitation before he replied whether he was sure he could object ; Stirrup was nct a man he should have recommended if be (Mr. Hunt) had been applied to first. Mr. Hunt handed a note to the magistrate which he had received that morning. but which he thought would not influence the application. That being ' so, Mr. Browne said he did not think the note should have been handed in, and asked who it was from. Mr. Hunt stated that the note was from the Town Clerk. Stirrup had never been before the War- rington court for any offence against the licensing laws in any way, but still in a good measure he felt he might have grounds to object. He thought the duty of object ing really belonged to him (Mr. Hunt). He was not so strong on points of law as to legally obj.ct, and as the grounds were somewhat slight he did not wish to press t. He had done his duty in mentioning it to the magis- trates ; if he had not done so he would have neen sure to have heard of it at the transfet day. Mr. Browne said Stirrup had married Mrs. Jones, of the Currier's Arms • she had ample experience. In regard to what Mr. Hunt had been saying it was all on hearsay.—Mr. Monks, one of the magistrates, said he could see that Mr. Hunt had great hesitation in his mind, and that the objection did not arise from heresay. Mr. Hunt had done quite right in mentioning to the Bench his reasons for hesitation. Alderman Fra.ncomb remarked that Stirrup had no conviction against him, and Alderman Harrison pointed out that there being nothing specific before them they would be doing nothing unjustifiable in granting the application.—Mr. Browne said that the real test would come on, on the transfer day.—The mag- istrates thereupon granted the application.

WAITING FOR DEATH. --- TRAGIC EXPERIENCE IN A HOSPITAL

SENSATIONAL STATEMENTS CONFIRMED.

An extraordinary affair is reported in the "Lincoln Gazette," as the result of an interview with a Mrs. Thomas Coney, of West Torrington, near Wragby. Mrs. Coney's experiences began with an illness, some two years ago, when she was nearly choked, owing to what her skilled medical attendant called (in the local phrase) a "full throat." She WM also distracted with pain in the back and between the shoulders. For three months she was medically attended, being in terrible pain the whole time, and she became conscious of a gradual shrinking and loss of flesh. During the whole of this time every morning she vomited a quantity of blood. The doctor could not believe she would ever recover so terribly thin and worn had she become, but the best advice he could give was that she should go into the Lincoln County Hospital. He said she had a bad liver, a diseased lung, and an internal tumour. Several times she was watched over by her friends, who thought she was taking her last breath. Acting on the doctor's re- commendation, Mrs. Coney was eventually conveyed in a carriage to the Hospital. No improvement taking place after a month's kind and assiduow treatment there, Mrs. Coney wrote to her husband to fetch her home, saying that she

Mr. J. REDMOND on Monday evening, in the House of ' Commons, moved, as an amendment to the motion for ' the Address in reply to the QUEEN'S Speech—" That the time has come when it is the duty of your Majesty's 1 Ministers to advise your Majesty to dissolve the present Parliament and submit the question of Home Rule tothe electors of the United Kingdom." He said the policy ' enunciated in the cry that "Ireland bkcks the way "had absolutely disappeared, and its place had been taken by the feeble cry that the cup of the Lords must be filled up. In the past three years there had been a change of policy and attitude towards the Fl..me Rule question on thepart of the Liberal party and the present Government, and he wished to remind them that they lost more elections now that they did not put that question to the front than when they did an. In his opinion the starting of the House of Lords agitation, meant the indefinite postponement of Home Rule because the House of Lords question was a much snore important one in the eyes of the British people, and could not he settled without many Parliaments being dissolved and many Governments falling. Mr. MORLEY said no more mischievous blow had been struck at Home Rule than this amendment. The pledge of the Government had been amply fulfilled; yet the hon. member said that a Government that had made such enormous strides with the question of Home Rule was to be Dbiected by the friends of Horne Rule and he desired to eject this Government by the aid Of the party who regarded the Home Rule as trea- son. HEALEY said !Mr. BALFOUR believed that by supporting the amendment he could ' strit- a deadly blow at Home Rule. Was not that a sufficient reason why Irishmen should not vote for the amendment? He believed that the best way te win Home Rule was to stick to the party which was trying to win it for them, and to oppose the party which , refused it. LECTURE ON THE EIAND CAMERA AND ITS CAPAIiILI CIES.

A meeting of the M arrington Amateur Photographic .slociety was held at the Museum Lecture Room on Tues- day evening, to hear a lecture by Mr. F. 0. Bynoe, of the Fuqua Hand Camera Co., London, upon the modern

hand camera, and its cap tbi'lities. The 'members of the society were invited to tiring their friends, and as a resul a good number availed themselves of the opport- unity. The chairman was Mr. H. N. Houghton, who also manipulated the lantern during the ever.ing. After he had been brit fiy introduced to the audience by Mr. Houg hton, _ _

Mr. BYNOYS at once proceeded with his subject. He said that to 1,,a via to sketch from nature WAS the work ot a life time, but nothing was simpler than picture mak- ing by photography. The taking of the subject would teach one to more closely observe, and consequently more keenly to enjoy the beauties which were round about. The inventor's ingenuity had been engaged until at last the hand camera was so simple, and at the same tiMe so effective that all that picture making re- quired was% steady band. With the camera the tra- veller could preserve many reminiscensesof his journeys, and the scientist and architect could also use them with advantage- The weight of the camera was beirg reduced to a minimum. All the paraphernalia f material was being compressed into a little cabinet to be carried in the hand. The introducion ot films was a great advance upon the glass plate. The &A real advance in the use of films in the changing camera was the introduction of the rall- able film in which the separate exposures were given by turning a roll. It was, however, thought that if the films could be placed in the camera already cut, similar tithe glass plate, a great advantage would be obtained. The problem before the inventor was to dis- cover a method whereby the expos.d film could be separated from the others. A number of devices were tried, and appeared to act very well, until they got into the hands of the public who had a worn-Jet-int knack of finding out the weak places in anythit g. (Laughter ) The difficulty in separating the 11M14 lay in theirextreme thinness, and should they vary the least in thiekness, al- most the whole of the appliances failed. Mr. By floe mere explained seteral likely plans which had been tried, and which had proved failures. He said that all devices in which one film was made to slide over the others -were bound to b. unsuccessful, for several reasons. Films sometimes became slightly adhesive. The celluloid film was strongly electric,,and could be excited very easilv by friction and would thus adhere to articles near to. The effect of this in the camera he was sure they would all realise. The only method likely to be successful with films was the gravity method whereby the exposed film was made to fall either to the bottom,or the back rj the camera, and this was the method adopted by the Frena Company. Their method was as follows: E tell film was cat with notches along the side. The cards which separ • abed the fi me were also cut with notchcs,the project ions of which corresponded with the notches in the film. When the pack of films were placed in the hnhier or dark slide, a ro-v of pins projected over, the preject ions of films, and when a film had been exposed all that Wae necessary was that a handle which was on the onside of the camera should be turned ?mind. This caused the row of pine to go forward and come over the interstices, and there now being no support, the film fell down into the receiver, and another film came inzo position. The Frena camera would hold 40 films, and an index on the outside of the camera told how many exposures had been made. A great advantage of these cut films over _

the roller film was that they could be taken out of the cameraany time without the trouble of cutting,and thus westing a portion of the film by joining it again to th., roller. The lecturer then described the shutter which was a very ingenious one, and which could be timed from one-fifth to one-eigh,ieth of a second. He believed there was no hand camera but the Frena, which had a swing back. This was provided for in the Frena by the handle for discharging the films. In many pictures it 11411 absolutely necessary to tilt the camera, and if the swing back were not then used, the upper portisn of the picture would be somewhat arched. In the Frena, the handle was provided with a spirit level, and whet the camera was tited, it was only necessary to bring the handle forward until it was perpendicular, and the film would then also be perpendicular. Of course the lens must be stopped down. Tne lecturer then went on t. describe the theory of the fixed focus lens which be did very lucidly. He said that, the Frena could take objects less than 20 feet by means of magnifiers which were pr - vided to correct the locus to any distance less than 20 feet. In conclusion, the lecturer said that he knew of nothing more interesting than picture taking while travelling about by means of which one could bring home memorials of one's travels which otherwise w,mll e lost in oblivion. A number of photographs were then tihrown on the screen, which were snepsho's taken by the Frena. At the close of the lecture, a vote of thanks twas acc rded the lecturer for his interesting lecture. Before the audience separated. Councillor Monks ex- hibited a number of slides which he had taken with a Prena camera during his recent tour round the world.

The cheapest method of living at the present time in this country is that of the Poles in the East-end Of Lon- doe, who, many of them, exist on a penny a day.
