DISTRESS IN WARRINGTON.

Sir,—Kindly allow me through the medium of your paper to draw the attention of the public of Warrington to the distress which has through bad trade and the severe weather been so prevalent in our midst for a long time past. At the time I write many work-people are experiencing the deepest poverty owing to their inability (in many instances through no fault of their I own), to follow their usual employment. The object I which I have in view in writing about this matter, is, to remind the workingclasses of the existence of a fund , subscribed to by tradespeople and others of our town, during the latecoal strike, and intended for their express benefit during such times as those we are at present pass- ing through. The Committee,organiv.d to superintend the proper administration of this fund, have, I understand, a substantial sum placed to their credit in the Bank at the present time. Knowing this to be the case allow me to suggest that the time has now come for this money to be administered to the wants of our suffering townspeople by providing soup kitchens, for their benefit. If the committee are waiting for worse times to appear before making use of the money, then I think they will have to wait a long time to have their desire gratified. Hoping that others will take an interest in this matter.—Yours respectfully, WILLIAM DIGGLE.

PASTEURISM AND HOMO] OPATAY. Sir,—ln my last letter I tried to show that the law re opposites was nature's method of cure as against the method of M. Pasteur: In answer to this Mr. Peers said "and homeeopathy seems to have no existence for him." By this I understand that he considers homceo- pathy as a sort of set-off to nature's law of opposites, because in atrather sentence he says of me that " his method the pre-Bacontan one of imagining a set of facts' avid then arguing for ar agair st them"—whereas the facts of my letter are as old as the hills. With regard to that invention of Flalinemann's " Like cures like," or "medicines cure what they cause" is no more tenable than Pasteurism itself. To quote Lord Bacon it is a case of " Idola Specus. the illuGions of a man's own den, bred out of his peculiar nature, habits, and pursuits." Talking of cure, we do not always think what cure 'm'ans. The word runs off our tongue as if it were some simple jet) of journeymen's work of no importance which any one can do. The simplest form of cure may be such as a newly cut finger required. How shall we dress it ? Well, it de- pends in a great measure upon whom the owner of the finger may be. If a shoe maker, he will put on it a plaister of wax, if a butcher it will be tied up with rag, and a quantity of salt, if a French polisher some polish, if a painter some knotting will be put upon it, and thus we may go on; and it is no matter which of these • methods:is adopted, the finger gets well, and is soon forgotten. Now then I ask what has cured the finger? The wax? No ! The salt ? No ! The polish ? No! The knotting? No ! What then did these things do ? They tended to keep the air and cold from the cut and nature did the rest. Let us scrutinise the work. We remember how the finger bled, that was because the knife had parted some blood vessels. These had to be made good. We have not forgotten how it pained, it smarted because the nerves were damaged, well, these nerves bad to be made all right ; and some new skin with its wonderful pores had to be laid over all ; and the whole to be endowed with life. Al this means a new creation which requires the exercise of that great Power that has made all things. Nothing less could have healed the cut finger ; nothing less can cure anything. Nature alone can make ; she alone can mend. No matter what doctor may be employed, nor to what medical school he may belong, his ignorance is often overceme by nature, and the patient cured in spite of the doctor's blunderings. In this way M. Pasteur or Hahnemann may receive credit which neither had earned. Mr. Kenyon in his letter on Pasteurism instanced several persons who had been bitten by a dog said to be rabid according to the evidence of "Dr.Barrow,of Liver- pool, and Professer Horsley, of Lon6en. The sufferers were sent to Paris to be treated,end thrugh many years have elapsed, not one of them has died of hydrophobia, and it is almost certain that some of them would have done so ere thig, had they not undergone the preventa- tive treatment." I have no doubt th it this is regarded by the learned writer as an absolute fact,yet I see plenty of room to doubt them. The dog in question may not have been mad, but only suffering f 7CIM toothache or some other bodily torment which would have caused him to conduct himself strangely and even suspiciously, and a very little would bring upon the poor creature cruel persecution and swift destruction Would not his extreme nervous excitement cause the brain to present such abnormal appearances as would warrant a profes- sional man in certifying that the dog ,v Is mad? Again, all the persons who are bitten by those who are un- doubtedly mad do net die. .In a village where I jived many years since, rabies broke out very badly. Many persons were bitten and many dogs. At One farm called Woodside Farm three cows went mad, and some horses that had been bitten had to be destroyed. I saw one young woman about eighteen years of age whose face arms and bands were fearfully bitten by a rabid cat. Her wounds had just crusted over when I saw her, and never have I seen such a horrid sight, for the parts of her face untouched were few and far between. "has anything been done for you, to save your life ?" I asked. "Yes, I've been dipped." I said, "What do you mean?" Here her mistress came to our help, and said, "All our people who have been bitten or had anything to do with the mad cattle have been dipped in the river Severn." This was firmly believed in. On in- quiry I found that in all the village there had been about thirty persons dipped in the SE vern as a preven- tative to rabies. I lived there four more years, but no deaths occurred amongst the bitten ones. This dipping had much to recommend it, which is s4dly wanting, in the dirty treatment of M. Pasteur.—Yours truly, FELIX SUMMERS.

THE INDUSTRIAL PROBLEM; ITS CAUSE AND

REMEDY.

Sir,—Kindly permit me through the medium of your valuable paper to draw attention to the great industrial problem confronting the workers of every grade of society to-dayttogether with its cons( quent awful soda results, with a view of eliciting from your many readers suggestions indicative of a permanent solution. Causes innumerable and remedies galore have of late appeared in newspap• rs , journals, magazines, and periodicals, of eevry descrip- tion. Professors and students in p)litico-economic science have inundated current labour literature with treatises enlivened in many instances by a persistent advocacy of doctrines pre-eminent on for their utter fallaciousness ; wise legislators have ii themselves of heaps of sympathetic rhetoric ; phillathropists have swelled the funds of Charity Organitstion Societies to huge dimensions—all in an effort to remove permanently and effectively the economic evils cf which modern society is characteristic to-day. Now, I venture to submit that if these evils are t'- rand the rlneu nf hllman control. thm Sonictc muqt

Wasting may result simply in a cough or cold, or weak lungs, or loss of flpsh. or it may go further and cause con- sumption and death. In any condition of wasting take Scott's Emulsion to restore solid flesh, strength and health, It is palatable and effective. (7)

===== 0002601_18950216_art0100.txt | included=true | included =====
continue to drag on its miserable existence until such time as natural law or Providence dissolve the earth and annihilate a society so destitute oF the means of self sup- port or improvement ; but if the evils are within the range of human control, then society must task itself to trace the causes which hamper and fetter the true pro- gress and social development of the race in the great march of civilisation and to sweep into eternal oblivion the barriers and obstacles accruing from the destruc- tive operations. What are the evils ? Society is divided to-day into classes with interests separate, distinct and hostile one to another. One class, the industrial, the wealth producers, own absolutely nothing but the sheer force of labour in their bodies. The other class, the privi- leged, the non-producers, own the very means essential to human life including the means of wealth production, distribution and exchange which comprise chiefly all the land, mines, minerals, railways, canals, shipping, machi- nery, &c., &c. Now, as anything which constitutes weath is the manifest embodiment of human labour exerted upon land aided by the other means of produc- tion, it is obviously undeniable that access to these must he gained before wealth can be produced, and as the class enjoying private appropriation of the means of pro- duction assert their claim to the wealth when produced, such wealth being fortified and sanctioned by modern laws, which are bound to be properly and advantage- ously interpreted for them by those of their class who administer the law, such access can only be obtained under conditions involving degradation to the industrial class. Hence the peculiar phenomena of the direct poverty in the midst of abundance of wealth. Under the private property system the renewed application of science to industry, every fresh invention which facili- tates and abridges labour simply aggravates and intensi- fies the evils by the displacement of humanlabour which the law of division and sub-division of labour admits of. Capital also as a consequence is undergoing a gradual process of concentration which still further widens the great gulf between the classes. The gradations that used to exist under more simpla conditions of labour have practically broken down, and a huge and hungry proletariat are face to face with a comparatively small but powerful capitalist class. The fierce struggle fer greater security among the dominant class, which receives additional momentum from the division of labour, which by grouping hunereds of Men tog,ether,each performing but an infmiteismal portion ot his craft, involve greater and more immense aggrega- tions of capital, crushes those who cannot compete with them, pitches them headlong into that terrible abyss below and they are delivered over naked to the furies of a competitive conflict which becomes keener and keener —that again involving greater riches for the few sur- vivors at the expense of the increased helplessness of the many. To reveal the stupendous changes wrought by science upon industry,figures must be resorted to, mere rhetoric being absolutely unavailable. The statistics are mainly American they being mere accessibleland complete. In the timber business 12 labourers with a Bucker machine will dr.ss 12,000 staves. The same number of men by hand labour would have dressed in the same time only 2,500. In the manufacture of paper a machine used for drying and cutting, run by four men and six girls, will do the work formerly done by 100 persons and do it much better. In tha manufacture of wall-paper the best evi- dence puts the displacement in the proportion of 100 to 1. In a phosphate mine in South Carolina 10 men accomplish with machinery what 100 men handle with- out it in the same time. There has b-en a displacement in rubber boots and shoes of 50 per cent. In South Carolina pottery, the product is 10 times greater by machine processes than by muscular labour. In the manufacture of saws experienced men consider that there has been a displacement of 3 men out of 5. In the weaving of silk the displacement has been 95 per cent. and in the winding of silk 90 per cent. A large soap manufacturing concern carefully estimates the displace- ment cf labour in its works at 50 per cent. In making wine in California a crushing machine has been intro- duced with which one man can caish and stem 83 tons of grapes in a day representing an amount of work form- erly requiring 8 men. In woollen goods mod, rn machinery has reduced muscular labour 33 per cent, in the carding department; 50 per cent in the spinning and 25 per cent in the weaving. In some kinds of spinning 100 to 1 re- presents the displacement. In the whole United States , in 1886 the machinery was equal to 3,500,000 horse power. If men only had been employed it would have required 21,000,000 to turn out the actual total product; the real number was 4- millions. To do the work accent- pushed in 1886 in the United States by power machinery and on the railways would have required men represent- ing a population of 172,500.000. The actual population of the United States in 1886 was something under 60,000,000 or a little more than one-third. The effect of labour-saving machinery under the private property system in Lancashire is too patent to necessitate com- ment. We see, therefore, that the root of the difficulty is economic. What then is the remedy ? The desperate and frantic effort of the human intelligence to fathom the apparent complexities of the problem have produced supposed remedies as remarkable for their utter ineffec- tiveness as for their varied character. Some attribute Poverty to over-production others to over-consumption. Both, however, are disposed of by the fact that neither can logically be said to exist until all human wants are satisfied. dome attribute it to unthriftiness, others to intemperance, some to want of better land laws, others toover-population. Some contend that British statesmen should concentrate their efforts to obtain our national supremacy in point of commerce. Rat it is possible for us to have with us simultaneously with that great achievement the direst poverty. Seine are anxious that the army should he strengthened and some addition made to the number of our battle-ships. But the strength and glory of a nation is not in standing armies and iron- clad fleets but in the happiness, welfare,and contentment of its people. We have at last arrived at an epoch when the true remedy can be no longer obscured. It is significant that the class most interested in the change are at last awakening from their apathy and somewhat lengthy stupor. The exploitation of the workers has at last become so flagrant and glaring that the disguise that hitherto disguised the true remedy has fallen off. The conscious and intelligent worker is, like the thirsty traveller in the desert, at the sound of the ripple of dis- tant waters, suddenly seized with indescribable feelings of joy as he perceives approaching in the dim distance the guardian angel whose mission is to disband his fetters and set him free. That angel is Social Democracy, and so rapid has her progress recently been and her influence se irresistible that her principles are acknowledged to be sacredly imperative to the welfare of the human race. Private property condemns the most useful class to a life of degrading toil. Socialism would vindicate the dignity of labour and place the worker in that position to which be is entitled by every natural law. Private property appeals to the worst passions in man and makes him a brute and a beast. Socialism would feder- ate men in common unity, brotherhood and love, would kindle in his heart the noblest impulses and the grandest side of human nature, would assert itself and spring out in every beautiful creed. Private property offers a distinct incentive to crime, murder, battle, corruption, prostitution and every description of vice. Socialism would !sweep away every vistage of evil that gave them birth. Ycurs truly,

WILLIAM GEE

MODERN "HUMANITY."

A STRONG INDICTMENT.

we of the nineteenth century are very self-compla- cent. We get up our societies for the prevention of cruelty to animals ; but it seems to me that by "animals" we mean only quadrapeds, and not many of them. Yet we let the birds drop dead od hunger under our very windows, and we slaughter without stint or mercy any feathered thing that we can reach, and the rarer it is the more fiercely we pursue it to its extinction. And it's all in the interests of science. Of course it is ! Verily, Mistress Science has much to answer for. It's all for science that young prigs on the sly take a slice off a dog's brain and let him run ; breed guinea-pigs till they inherit the faculty of feeding on their own toes ; give a criminal a touch of an electrical machine to see how long he takes to die ; or treat everything that flies as no better than a specimen to set up on wire legs in a glass case,or to pin down to a cork, , and boast, "We've got him at last !" I doubt whether we are as kind to dumb creatures as the ancients were. —Dr. Jessop.

GENERAL NEWS.

Frost bells are) tolled in some districts of France when frost is threatened. Immediately the inhabitants place quantities of tar between the rows of vines. The tar is lighted, and volumes of dense smoke arise, thus pro- tecting the vines. The Quakers in Great Britain only amount to about eighteen thousand, and could all be contained in the Agricultural Hall, at Islington. They are represented in the House of Commons by e;ght memb.rs. This is a very large representation as compared with other deno- minations. .

The body of an American saloon passenger named A. E.Lockhart—one of those who lost their lives in the Elbe disaster—has been landed at Margate. Another body, apparently that of a lady of position, has been landed at the same place.

TEE EXAM NER. SATUROAY, FEBRUARY 16 1895 NOTES ON GAMES AND I EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON. ATHLETICS.

FOOTBALL. eiRICKBTERS should acq lire the "Pocket Diary and Companion " issued by the 'well-known Sports Manu- factures, George G. Bussey it Co., Peckham, London. All the information Cricketers require is found therein, as well as the usual Diary, yet it will go in the waistcoat pocket Leather bound, gilt edges, 6d., postage id. It you are not satisfied G. G. 13. & Co. will return the price. There was another blank, so far as Rugby football was concerned, last Saturday. In fact not a single game of any importance took place in Great Britain. This has been a winter of records, and this is probably a record in this respect. At any rate it lea very long time since Such a complete cessation of football occurr- ed before. We have not yet got to the end of the frost either, so that there is a possibility of the same thing happening again to-day.

Warrington has been very unfortunate, and were especially so on Saturday. The fixture with Oldham, is the most popular one on the card, and would have drawn a monstre gate. The Oldhamers occupy a similar position in Lancashire Rugby football, as Sunderland do in the Association code, and almost invariably draw big gates. There appears to be some strange fatality with regard to their fixtures with Warrington. It has scarcely ever happened that the fixtures in one season could both be played, for almost invariably one has had to be can- celled owing to the weather. Both were played last season, but the match at Oldham was practically a farce as regards the play, for the weather was fearful'. It has previously 13.:en Oldham who have been the sufferers from the freaks of the weather, but now Warrington have had their turn.

Warrington are due to play the return match with Broughton Rangers, to-day, but there is not n. uch probability of the game being brought off, and it will most likely suffer the same fate as the rest of the 1895 fixtures. Should the match be played, the following team will do duty for Warrington : J. Boscow, full back; Barber, Heesom, Moores, and Burton, three- quarters ; Bate and Nevins, half-backs ; Turner, Jolley, Dakin, Taylor, Berry, Bailey, Barrett and Houghton, forwards.

The Association game among the first-class clubs has not been much interfered with by the weather, and on Saturday the contest for the Lancashire Cup was ad- vanced a stage, and now there are only four left for it. These are Liverpool, Everton, Preston North End, and Accrington. In the semi-finals, Liverpool or Everton must fall, and there will no doubt be a tremendous crowd to witness the match between these local rivals. As Everton have won and drawn with Liverpool this season, it is now the latter's turn to win, but this is not at all likely, for Everton appear to have recovered their form again. Preston North End should easily polish off Accrington, and the final should be between them and Everton. Everton are the present holders of the cup, and will make a determined effort to keep it.

Sunderland have lost the single point with which they had an advantage over Everton in the League competi- tion, by only making a draw with Small Heath on Saturday. It is singular that the " Heathens" have drawn with each of the three leading clubs in the League in their home matches, the scores being : Everton 4-4, Aston Tilla 2-2, and Sunderland 1-1. Everton have now again a chance of winning the championship, but they have two difficulties yet in the way. They have an inferior goal average to Sunderland, and also have to go to Sunderland to play that team, to say nothing about the Aston Villa fixture. It is, therefore, probable that they will finish the season a very geod second to the 'Wearsiders.

The English Cup ties are resumed to-day, and some very tough struggles are to be expected. No fewer than 1.3 clubs out of the sixteen engaged are first division clubs. As the remaining three who are not in the First League are drawn away from home' it looks as though the remaining stages will be fought by the senior clubs alone. The two most interesting fixtures are Everton v. Blackburn Rovers, and Sunderland v. Preston North End. Looking at the fixtures all round, the probability is that the home team will win on almost every occasion, though West Bromwich Albion and Bury are favourites, notwithstanding that they are away from home. 1.0.G.T. (EBENEZER LODGE). The usual weekly session of the Earlestown Ebenezer Lodge, No. 752, was held in the Co-operative Hall, on Tuesday night. The pregramme of the evening was as follows :—Bro. _ CONCERT AT BURTONIVOOD.

A night of wit and humour' was spent in the Board Schools Burtonwood on Sunday evening, the contribu- tors to the pleasantness heir.g a party of Liverpudlians introduced by Mr. W. T. Sutton.—Taking into considera- tion the fact that Burtonwood is situate in the heart of the country ' three miles from everywhere,' and Jack Frost 'was holding undisputed sway, there was a fairly numerous audience, who patiently awaited the some- what late arrival of the artistes. This however was fully made up for by the heartiness with which the singers performed the tasks they had set themselves. —Several of the artistes notably Mr. George Aspinall, Mr. Chapman, add Mr. MacHermott, judging 1 from the reception they received, are old and welcome I favourites at Burl,Anwood, and the manner in which they subsequently acquitted themselves fully justified the warm appreciation of the audience. With one or two exceptions the concert was of a humorous character, all the songs being of a nature which kept the audience in a continual state of merriment. The exceptions were the songs "The Toilers," and " The Star of Bethlehem," which were given with remarkable finish and taste by Miss Hetty Mawdsley, whose distinctive features are a rich soprano voice and clear enunciation, and she was rapturously applauded • selections on the mandoline and guitar by Messrs. W. and A.McFarlaud,who proved themselves capable musicians, and their contributions were redemancied.—The descriptive song " Kimberley " was finely interpreted by Mr. W. B. White, and he was also fairly successful in the song" Tommy Atkins.—Mr. J. MacHermott's contributions were most amusing, his comicalities placing the audience in convulsions. He is popularly known as" the Liverpool Chevalier," a title evidently well-merited. His " Peculiarities," (in charac- ter) " A Musical Party," and " By the Sad Sea Waves," were remarkably well-given.—Mr. Matt. Covner proved himself a clever artiste,his humorous songs " Oh ! what a night it must have been," and "Ours is a happy home," bringing forth rounds of applause.—As a mimic, Mr. George Aspinall was well to the fore, and his songs " Jook McCraw," and "Forty Twa," received the proper amount of recognition.—Mr. Chapman fully justified his reputation as a reciter, the descriptive story of " How Bill Adams won the battle of Waterloo," was so quaintly given as to excite the utmost mirth, while the monologue "The Alarm," was exceptionally well treated. The other items were "Me and Her "and "The Dandy Coloured Coon," Mr. J.W.Wright, " Simple Simon," and " The Irish Schochnaster," by Mr. W. Martin were very amusing and cleverly caricatured. At the close hearty thanks were accorded the enter- tainers, and Mr. Sutton veey suitably acknowledged the compliment. The piano accompanists were Miss MacDermott, Messrs. W. T. Sutton and Mr. George Kidd.

SONS OF TEMPERANCE.

Foden, Judson, Clare, Pickett, and Pearson taking part. A vote of thanks was accorded to Bro. Clarke for his paper. CONSERVATIVE BALL AT EARLESTOWN.

The annual ball promoted by the Earlestown Conser- vative Club took place on Friday evening in the Town Hall, Earlestown. The room had been tastefully decor- ated for the occasion, and wheu the dancers, who num- bered about 60 couples, were assembled, the mingled, colours of the ladies dresses,made the scene an animated one. Mr. Peplow's excellent string band supplied the music, and dancing was carried on with much spirit until about half-past four on Saturday morning. Among the prominent Conservatives present were Messrs. C. B. F. Borron. CC,, and Robert Stone. The catering,which was of a satisfactory character, was in the hands of Mr. R. Lythgoe, and the general arrangements were effi- ciently carried out by the committee of the club. NEWTON CONGREGATIONAL SUNDAY SCHOOL. The annual senior scholars' party was held in the Congregational schcolroom, Newton, on Saturday even- ing. About 60 sat down to a tea which was satisfac- torily catered for by Mrs. King,and thoroughly enjoyed. After tea the number present was increased to about 100, and the evening was very pleasantly spent in sing- ing and games. The following very ably contributed to the harmony of the proceedings :—Miss Randall, who sang "Stop thief ;" Mr. R. Owen, " The young brigade ; Miss Bristow, " Flight of ages ;" Mr. W. Owen, "Yeoman's wedding song :" Miss Mather, " Genevieve ;" Mr. G. Kidd, " Holy City ;" Miss U.quhart, "Children asleep." Mr. Thompson presided at the piano.

TRH FROST. In this neighbourhood the frost still holds sway, and every pool of water in the district is frozen over.—Pipes and taps innumerable, both hot and cold water con- veyers, are blocked, and the timorous have been forced to seek safety in front sitting rooms, leaving the kitchen with its nemesis in shape of a boiler, to the tea. der mercies of the frost. At Newton lake thousands of people have indulged in skating during the week, and there have been numerous skating matches, cricket matches, and hockey have also been played, and the week, to skaters, has been one of perfect enjoyment.— There have been one or two accidents caused by skaters colliding, one which happened on Wednesday night being of a rather serious nature, inasmuch as the victim was rendered4unconscious, and medical assistance had to be obtainad.—Great difficulty is experienced in keep- ing the canal clear, the ice boats having to make daily visits to remove the nightly accumulation of ice. SCARCITY OF WATER AT EARLESTOWN. Since Friday last, the supply of water in 'the higher parts of Earlestewn, has been of a very indifferent char- acter, and for several days something like a famine existed. The frost was to some extent responsible for this, as on examination it was found, that although the usual quantity of water was being sent through the pipes, a large quantity was running to waste owing to a leakage caused apparently by the bursting of one of the mains. Great inconvenience was experienced by the inhabitants, especially in Crow-lane and Cross-lane, where not a drop of water could be obtained. On Sun- day a water cart promenaded the town, and dispensed water at the houses, the inhabitants eagerly crowding round for a bucketful. This was continued on Monday and Tuesday, and by this means, some alleviation of the inconvenience was made. The supply has now reached its normal condition, and where the house pipes are not frozen, no difficulty is experienced in supplying the domestic wants. SALFORD HARRIERS AT CULCIIIITII. The cross-country champions visited the " Harrow" Inn on Saturday, and were joined there by the Leigh Harriers, who were strengthened by the inclusion of Messrs. Lewis, Marier, Morris, and Waywell, from Earlestown. About 3.30 the hare, Oscar Quigley and J. H. Lewis representing Leigh, and Dunn of Salford, started to lay a trail through Culcheth Hall grounds (Squire Withington having given permission) and across the fields in the direction of Pennington, until nearing the railway. Travelling along the metals for a mile th fields were again struck, until coining to the Glazebury highway, from which point nearly three miles of road were negotiated. The order of arrival was as follows: Salford Morton 1, Such 2, F. Johnson 3, Lind 5, Redmond 6, Cooper 7, total 24 points. Leigh : Hor- rocks 4, Glover 8, Way well 10, Marler 13, Winstanley 14, Rothwell 15, total 64 points. The total distance covered was about 61 miles, and the Salfordians won easily by 40 p )ints. The run through the crisp dry air was much enjoyed, and put all in good form for the repast afterwards provided. A musical eveniag, in ' which Messrs. Lewis and Quigley shared the honours, terminated a pleasant outing. EARLESTOWN CO-OPERATIVE SOCIETY. The 137th quarterly meeting of the Earlestown Co- operative eSoecngiienty: m Wars. TON.

The frost in Warrington and the surrounding district continues with great severity and as a consequence, large numbers of people ha velbeen thrown out ef work. There has, however, .been an at most unprecedented opportunity fo. indulging in skating, numerous fields about the district having been flooded. A fine piece of ice has been in use at the Twiggery farm, Man- chester-road, and on Saturday, Mr. Newton, the tenant, kiddly handed over the management of the whole day's proceedings to a committee for the object of aiding the Infirmary. We are glad to report the following splendid results :—Gross takings, £l9 14s. ; less labourers' wages for clearing the ice, £1 is. ; and sundry expenses, 55., £1 6s. ; nett takings, £lBB9. This will be banded over to the Warrington Infirmary. The prizes were given by various tradespeople of the town, the cost of printing being defrayed by Mr. Councillor Shaw. Sixty compe- titors entered for the various events, and some very ex- citing racing resulted. Messrs. J. D. Brimelow. E. Hope, and G. Molyneux officiated as handicappers ; Mr. William Wood as starter ; Mr. Councillor Evans. Mr. Councillor Shaw, and Mr. J. T. Whittle, as judges, and a strong committee of workers headed by Mr. Alderman Wright, J.P., did yeoman service. Too much praise cannot be given to Mr. William Habberley for the able manner in which he discharged his heavy duties as hon. secretary. The men's handicap (200 yards) was arrang- ed in ten heats, three competing in each, the winners of the heats being Sergeant Beale, T. Eaves' J. Eaves, J. Griffin, E. Boscow, Reynolds, W. Butterworth, J. Slater, J. Draper, and T. Hackett ; and, after running the second round, Messrs. Beale (scratch), Boscow (10 yards start), Slater (15 yards start), and Hackett (20 yards start), were left to compete in the final for four prizes, resulting after an exciting race as follows :—E. Boscow. Ist prize, case of carvers given by the Work- ingmen's Infirmary Committee :J. Slater, .2nd, copper I kettle, given by Mr. Joseph Allison, of Manchester-road; Sergeant Beale, 3rd, half-dozen desert knivep, given by Mr. J. S. Garland, Bridge-st. ; T. Hackett, 4th, case of dessert spoons, given by Messrs. W. P. and W. Skel- ton, Ltd. In the youth's handicap (200 yards), twenty entries were divided into five heats, the winners of the heats being J. Nevins, G. Topping, T. Rigby, C. Povey, and F. Smith. In the final heat, after a grand race, the judges decided as follows :—G. Topping (50 yards start), Ist prize. gold centre medal, given by the Workingmen's Infirmary Committees ; J. Nevins (10 yard start), fancy penknife, given by Messrs. Caldwell' s Ltd. ; C. Povey (15 yards start), 3rd' Norwegian, given be Mr. J. D. Daintith, of Bridge-st. In the Consolation Handicap (200 yards) the competi- tors, numbering ten, had an exciting struggle for the three prizes,resulting as follows :—William Butterworth (20 yards start), Ist priz, 1 dozen knives and forks, given by Mrs. Brundrett, of Church-st. ; P. La wless (5 yards start), 2nd, nickel-plated teapot, given by Mr. William Wood, of Mr. Smith's Mineral Water Works ; Dr. Kisby (scratch), 3rd, 1 dozen tea spoons, given by Mr.H. N. Houghton, Market-gate. The spectators were well pleased with the splendid exhibition of speed- skating given by Dr. Kisby, Sergeant Beale, Messrs. Boscow, Griffen, Houghton, Hoskin., and the brothers Eaves. Mr. Alderman Wright, J.P., distributed the prizes to the successful competitors. Some excellent skatiag was obtained this week on a portion of the old river near Wilderspool Brewery. One young man, on Friday, had a narrow escape from being drowned. The ice broke, and he was immersed up to the waist, and would probably have been drowned had he not succeeded in catching hold of one of the many large pieces of timber which form a raft, and which have been lying in the river for some months past. On Friday and Saturday considerable difficulty was ex- perienced in keeping the Ship Canal open to navigation, As the rising tide brought 'enormous masses of floe ice, amounting to some thousands of tons, from the estuary of the river Mersey, through the tidal openings into the Ship Canal near Runcorn. The company's steam hoppers,Alpha and Reta,which were available for break- ing ice in the canal, and which attempted to release the Grampus, were also unable to get through the ice pack, and the company's powerful four-propellor dredger,Ma.n- chester, which was working in the upper section of the Canal, was requisitioned. The Manchester succeeded irs forcing a passage through the pack, and now the canal is open to traffic from end to end, the vessels which had been detained having resumed their journeys. The canal in this district, from the Stag Bridge at Walton to the kicks at Latchford, presents an unusual appearance, as hundreds of thousands of pieces of ice of all shapes, sizes and thicknesses are to be seen. There has been some excellent skating upon what is familiarly known as the Old Quay Canal at Walton ; where the ice, of a good quality, extends for about two miles. The Penieth Friends' School skating contests took place on Saturday, at Clambrook meadows. The con- tests were arranged by the kindness of Mr. J. S. Hodg- son, of Manchester, life member of the National Skating Association, and holder of second and third class badges of the association for figure skating. Mr. liodgson,who has been closely connected with the interests of the Friends' School,has for some years desired to inauguree these contests, but till the present severe frosthad been unable to do an. The events arranged were a race for the seniors, over 13, one for the juniors, and a figuring contest. Six competitors started for the first race, which was about a mile, including six sharp'turns. G. Cooke proved winner, though he was closely pressed in the last length by H. Bodmer, who was only five yards behind at the finish. The junior race for half the distance re- sulted in a victory for R. Lowe,J. Marsden being second Very few en•ered for the figuring contest, Mr. Hodgson soon induced several to attempt, setting them various simple figures and tricks to do, for which he gave num- bers according to the accuracy with which his instruc- tions were carried out. G. Cooke again prayed victor, H.l3odmer being runner up, though very far behind the winner.

BIIRTONWOOD SCHOOL BOARD.

A special meeting of the aboveio-a-i:d-Wa's held in the classroom of the Board Schools, Burtonwood, on Thurs- day, last week, when there were present Messrs. Application for the use of the school for a Diorama and for a ball were read and refused,and the clerk reported that he had let the schools as in pre- vious years to the Social Club for their annual concert. Plans for alterations and additions to the schools were laid before the Board, and after some discussion as to the title to the school premises the matter was postponed for the present. The Clerk was authorized to subnm oil all parents for irregular scholars, reported to him by the attendance officer, to appear before the Board to show cause why 'proceedings should not be taken against them. The, attendance officers' report was considered, from which it appeared that he had visited since the last meeting 232 cases of irregular attendance. Notice of pupil teachers' examination on the 28th April next, was read. •

SERIOUS FIRE AT NEWTON.

£1,200 DAMAGE. A fire having disastrous results broke out in the farm buildings of Mr. John Bridge, at Newton-le-Willows. The farm:is known as Parkside Farm, and is situated on the road from Preston Junction to Winwick, close to Newton-le-Willows. It belongs to Lord Newton, and is tenanted as stated above. Mr. Bridge on Sunday morning, had gone to church, and about 10.30 the servant vomit into the barn' and round the stacks in seareh of eggs, and observed nothing wrong. About twenty minutes after- wards, Mrs. Bridge happening to look out saw a quantity of smoke and fearing something was wrong, sent the servant out, who, seeing that it was coming from the stacks, loosed the cows and horses, and turned them adrift. A strong wind was blowing from the South- East, and very quickly the dutch barn, from where the smoke was seen to be issuing, was in a blaze. The barn was full of hay and straw, and a wagon load of hay standing between it and the shippons, taking fire,formed a connecting link, which carried the fire from the barn to the stables and shippons. A crowd of people soon gathered on the scene, and word was sent to Warrington for the fire brigade, which arrived at the farm a little after 12. Inspector Morgan, of Earlestown, was also notified, and he with Sergeant Long,worth and a couple of constables rendered what assistance they could. There was a plentiful supply of water at hand, and the brigade made good practice on the blazing buildings,but the fire had secured a firm hold, and, aided by the wind,. the flames could not be got under until the buildings were completely gutted. The firemen, however, were successful in preventing the fire from reacking the farm house, which escaped without injury.—ln addi- tion to the hay and straw, all the farming implements, including a wagon and spring cart were destroyed.— No cause can be assigned for the outbreak, but the servant girl states she saw two men going away from the direction of the farm about half-past ten, The damage, which is fully covered by insurance, is esti- mated at £1,200.

NOTES BY THE WAY AT EARLESTOWN.

(BY AN OCCASIONAL CONTRIDITTOR).

They were having high jinks in Newton last week end, I believe. From what I can gather, somebody became generous, and entertained those who had taken part in the work during the election to the District Council. Success is a wonderful thing to open a person's pockets, and I suppose those laugh best who win. Something in the shape of a supper was provided, and congratulations were general all round. Everybody's health was drunk, and if the expressions for success which were given vent to may be taken as any criterion,then Newton may look forward to a most successful career in the future. The affair I suppose was of a private nature, and it is not my province to deal with any of the pretty nothings which were uttered, but it is perhaps as well to bear such matters in mind, as no man knoweth what the future may bring forth. We know not what" surprises" may be in store. One thing we do know for a certainty, and that is that some of the Newton people have become re- markably discreet of late.

I fancy we must set about marshalling our forces, and that without very much delay. lam told that the election for a County Councillor is coming upon us with rapid strides. It is, perhaps, generally 'known that Mr. Borron is our present representative on the Council, and from what I can learn he has done good service for the district, and in consequence some people will say re-elect him. Those, however, are not my sentiments, and I hope the rumour that there is likely to be a contest will prove to be a fact. Mr. Borron has had his three years of office, and if the gentleman I have heard spoken of as a likely candidate is returned, we shall not by any meal's suffer by th 3 change. We are getting quite accustomed to changes, although some are for the better and others tor the worse, but in this instance I shall bail a contest with de- lightjand I don't suppose Mr. Borron will grumble. From what I know of him he would not care to be returned unopposed, as, to toy mind, an unop- posed election savours very much of apathy, and the true feeling of the electom is not brought to the surface. Therefore, I say, be up and doing, and let the best man win.

--- The frost still holds sway in this neighbourhood and skating is being pretty generally indulged in. To skaters, and those who are not troubled with hot water arrangements in their houses, hopes are expressed that the present condition of the weather will continue, and there is not the slightest doubt that its health giving qualities are without ques- tion. Householders, however, are complaining of frozen water pipes and gas meters,and consequently desire a spell of much milder wgather. Judging from the hopelessness depicted on the faces of these complainers, one is inclined to introduce a slightly altered text, " Blessed is he who bath not a boiler behind his kitchen fire, for then shall he suffer no anxiety,"—(copyright). The boiler explosion last week was a result of the frost, but I am thinking had it been a copper boiler no such disastrous effects would have occurred. I think there should be a law precluding builders from putting boilers behind kitchen fires, for in such weather as this if the utmost care is not taken, they are sources of the very greatest darager. .

I see the committee for the May Queen festival have commenced their deliberations in connection with this year's show. I don't wish to appear im- pertinent, but I hope such arrangements will be made this year that no possible fear of last year's fiasco being repeated can be entertained. Instead of a substantial gain, a regrettable loss had to be reported, and as the same committee has charge of affairs, I trust their experience on itbat occasion will have put them on their guard.

EARLESTOWN ORCHESTRAL SOCIETY.

ANNUAL CONCERT.

The first concert promoted by the. recently formed Orchestral Society was brought to a most successful issue in the Town Hall, Earlestown, on Thursday even- ing. There was a full audience, who were amply re- warded by a first class musical treat. The talent got together was quite out of the ordinary run of artistes who usually appear on Earlestown platforms, all,l the quality of the singing was much above the average. In fact it is long since such a refined musical evening has been enjoyed in Earlestown. The band which is com- posed entirely of local musicians, has made wonderful strides in the direction of proficiency lately, due no doubt to the skilled training they have received at the hands of Mr. A. Ashley, who has spared no efforts to make the Society a credit not only to himself, but the members also. The concert was opened by the band, with the overture, "La Souveraine," which was so ad- mirably given, as to place the audience at ease for the rest of the evening. They'closed the first part of the pro- gramme with selections from " The Gondoliers," in which the cornet solos, were excellently played ; the I trombone solos, were also given without a blemish, and altogether the performance was most enjoyable. The other items given by the band were, waltz " Casino-Tanze," and " War March of the Priests," the execution of which were in every way praise- worthy,and the audience showed their appreciation in a hearty manner. Whilst dealing with the instrumental- ists we might here mention the performance of Miss Bessie Grundy, whose manipulation of the violin, con- sidering she was a girl of only 11 years of age was ex- ceptionally clever, and she received a vociferous recall. Miss Annie Nelson (Australian Prima Donna), a soprano of no mean ability, was perfectly at home in the songs she contributed,viz. vocal waltz; " Carmena," and song, " Good-bye," her singing being of a high order, and finished in style. She received an enthusiastic reception and was heartily applauded. She also appeared to ad- vantage in the duet, " A night in Venice," with Mr. A. F. Thornborough, principal tenor, Chester Cathedral. Mr. Thornborough has an effective style, and his sing- ing of the ballad, "Tom Bowling," was given in a manner which left nothing to be desired. His other contribution was " No lips can tall," the excellent inter- pretation of which received the loud plaudits of the audience. Mr. John Sudworth, of Nottingham, but a native of Earlestown, surprised many of those present by the excellency of his singing, his rich bass voice being peculiarly adapted to the songs he submitted, " The Skipper," "In days of old," and "The Diver." for each of which he earned a hearty recognition. Mr. Ashley showed his proficiency as a musician in the clarionet solo " Alicante," which he gave in most perfect style, The humour of the evening was contributed by Mr. E. Whittle, of Bolton, who is too well known amongst Earlestown audiences to need comment. The name is a sufficient pass-word to merri- ment. His items were " Poor Mary Ann," and "The Village Concert," for each of which he received the usual encore. Mrs.Latimer creditably performed the onerous and difficult task of accompanist,and Mr. Ashley wielded the baton in the orchestral numbers. The success on this occasion will doubtless spur the society on to fur- ther and greater efforts in the future.

ST. HELENS THEATRE ROYAL.

This extraordinarily popular musical play, written by Mr. George Dance, author of "The Nautdi Girl," and "The Gay Parisienne," is to fpay a return visit to the Theatre Royal, St. Helens, next week. Originally pro- duced nearly two years ago at the Opera House, North- ampton,"The Lady Slavey"lias had an uninterrupted suc- cessful run in the provincial theatres of over 400 nights. Mr. William Greet, who produced the play at the Avenue Theatre, London,bas got together a strong Com- pany and every attention is paid to making a perfect ensemble both in regard to the principal characters and the chorus. The muse, chiefly composed by Mr. John Crook, is rendered with the fullest effects. Mr. Greet's business manager and trea- surer is Major George Stratton. In fact the company is fully equal in strength to Mr. Greet's other companies which have earned so wide a reputation for their rendering of" La Cigale "and "Little Christopher Columbus." "The Lady Siavey " was played in the Avenue Theatre to enormous receipts for a lengthy period, and has only just been withdrawn to make way for the musical burlesque, by Mr. George R. Sims, and composed by Ivan Caryll, entitled " Little Dick Whit- tington." " The Lady Slavey " and her retinue will arrive at the St. Helens station on Sunday next at 3 p.m., and all who desire to spend a happy evening should not fail to pay her a visit at the St. Helens Theatre Royal next week.

LYlifM.

ANNUAL TEA PARTY. —The annual tea party and en- tertainment in connection with the Sion Chapel, Cherry- lane, was given on Monday when a good number of persons partook of an excellent tea,admirably served by a few ladies. An entertainment succeeded this event, songs, recitations, instrumental music, Sze., being rendered in a capital manner by a combination of artistes. The chair was occupied by Mr. J. A. Swan- wick. STARTLING Occunnexce.—An accident occurred on Saturday, which was fortunately not attended with any very serious result. Mr. Jelin Wilson,cab pro- prietor, was driving two horses, attached to a convey- ance, towards the village by way of Booth's Hill. The horses suddenly became unmanageable, and Mr. Wilson, whilst endeavouring to see his young son safely to the ground, could not prevent the animals colliding with a lamp-post. The shock of the collision threw Mr. Wilson to the ground, and the horses being then free dashed at an alarming rate down Booth's Hill. At the foot of the hill they ran against the Post-offiee window, smashing one large pane of glass to atoms, and scraping the wood- work. The son of Mr. Wilson escaped without injury, but Mr. Wilson was severely shaken. One of the horses was hurt but not severely, and the 'bus was damaged. SATURDAY POPULAR CONCERTS.—There was a large at- tendance at the Newfield Institute an Saturday evening, at the usual popular concert. The programme had been undertaken by Mrs. Dodds, her husband. The opening piece was a pianoforte duet by Messrs. Wood and Sod- low, followed by songs by Mr. W. H. Wallington. Miss May Pierpoint sang with taste and expression " Home, Deane Home," and afterwards gave "Venetian song," for which she received a well-deserved encore, respond- ing with " Bonnie Loch Lomond." The next item was a humorous musical sketch by Mr. Alfred Grounds en- titled a" Juvenile party." He subsequently appeared in two ventriloquial sketches "The School Btard Beadle "and "Music and Mimicry," both af which were received with uproarious applause. Mr. R. Neville Fos- ter contributed two cleverly executed violin solos and Mr. Adcroft rendered a clarionet solo in a very skilful manner. Miss Ethel Gornall gave two excellent render- ings of the songs" Dawn "and "0 promise me." A recitation was given by Madam Ruhb entitled " Ask , Mamma," followed by a song by Mr. Levi Richardson, " Sally in our Alley," who later gave effectively " My pretty Jane." Mr. Fieldhouse again made a welcome appearance and sang in fine style" The Bandilero." Mr. W. Asquith Wood proved himself an able accompanist. The singing of the National Anthem brought a most en- joyable evening to a close. LYMAI CONGREGATIONAL SUNDAY SCHOOL.-011 Mon- day evening an entertainment of a refined and novel character was given before a good assembly in these schools, a a tableaux vivants, statuary, and movable waxworks, the proceeds being devoted to the Band of Hope Fund. The beauty ef the effects was supplemented by lime-light, powerfully shadowed upon the stage in many delicate shades of colour by Mr. Loudon. It is superfluous to describe in detail the figures represented, but it is no exaggeration to say that their draperies were the work of a genius, and reflected the greatest credit upon the Misses Ouston and Brearley, who had charge of them. The pieces represented in tableaux vivants included the stories of " Cinderella," in seven scenes ; "The Sleeping Beauty," ha five scenes; "Good morning! Have you used Pears Soap ?" and" The Wedding Morn- ing" of Sunlight Soap. In the Movable Waxworks four scenes were shown, viz: The Scene Continental "includ- ing "A Spanish Lady," " Dresden China," and " A Grecian Maiden," taken by the Misses Westgarth. Then " The fine Arts" described " Music "" Art, ' and " Literature," by the Misses Nightingale, Moston, and Brearley respectively. " A Miscellaneous Scene" depicted " Red Riding Hood," " Santa Claus," " Sweep " " Brittania," " Tichborn," " Where are you going to my pretty maid " (2 figures), "You dirty boy" (2 figures) and " Little Miss Moffit," the several subjects being taken by the scholars and teachers of the Sunday Schools. As a final waxwork scene there was shown "A Waxwork tea party," supposed to be taking place in the showman's absence from home—" When the cat's away," &c.; and a very funny effect was produced by what the showman described as" by an electric current turning on the laughing gas,"—all the figures being I made to join. Throughout the evening the audience ex- pressed their pleasure in audible term s,and encores were very frequent. Mr. J. R. Ormrod represented the char- acter of showman, and kept the auidence amused be- tween the brief intervals and changes of scene with many laughable ancedotes, some mimicry, and tunny extrava- gances ; and he was ably assisted in the Waxworks by the appearance of" Jonathan, the boy," in clownly get up, in the person of Mr. Jack Westgarth. Mr. William Lawrence rendered very able service as stage manager, and the absence of any hitch in an entertainment neces- sitating so much impromptu arrangement, speaks volumes for his ability. Mrs. Gotobed played selections of music during the course of the evening. At the con- clusion of a happy period the Rev. C. Clay,B.A.,moved, on behalf of the Band of Hope,a vote expressive of their appreciation, which was passed with acclamation.

PARISH CHURCH TEA MEETING.—In the Girls' schom . room on Friday evening, the usual congregational tea party in connection with the Parish Church was held. , A good number sat down to tea, after which a miscel- laneous programme of vocal and instrumental music , was submitted provided by the members of the choir who were assisted by Miss Boys, of Halton, and Mrs. -' Birkett, Billington and Clare from Runcorn. A drama- tic sketch was al=co provided,and taken part in by Misses ' Jeacock, A. and H. Acton,and Messrs. R. and F. Jeacock Paton, and Ellames. During an interval the Rey. G. D. 1 Wray (vicar) delivered an interesting address. TrayS , were given by Sir Richard and Lady Brooke, Mesdames ,•:' Cox, Evans, Carruthers, J.
